\chapter{Shared analytic bridges}
\label{chap:shared-analytic-bridges}

This chapter collects analytic statements shared by several proofs.  Listing a statement here
does not make it a premise of every proof.
The comparison bridges below are proved in Lean, including smooth/literature
equivalence under intrinsic absolute continuity and the general log-concave Cheeger--Buser
comparison.
A published input or an upstream reviewed proof receives no \texttt{leanok} merely from its citation.

\section{Three presentations of the Poincar\'e constant}

\begin{theorem}[Smooth tests and ambient relaxation have the same finite constants]
  \label{thm:legacy-relaxed-holds-equivalence}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.SmoothTest.holds_iff_ambient,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.SmoothTest.holds_iff_preEnergy}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:preenergy-poincare-relaxation,def:l2-mean-variance,def:smooth-gradient-preenergy,
        def:smooth-gradient-relaxation,def:legacy-smooth-test-poincare}
  Let $\mu$ be a probability law on a finite-dimensional real Hilbert space.  For every finite
  $C\ge0$, the smooth-test inequality on all globally smooth finite-gradient-energy tests holds at $C$
  if and only if the $L^2$ inequality for the ambient sequential relaxation holds at $C$.
  The raw smooth-gradient test family has exactly the same finite-constant predicate.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  A constant sequence bounds relaxed energy by raw test energy. Conversely, at each
  finite relaxed-energy point use a recovery sequence whose raw energies converge to that value.
  Continuity of $L^2$ variance passes the inequality to the limit for every finite $C\ge0$,
  including $C=0$. Infinite-energy points are outside the finite-energy test domain. No density
  or equality of raw and relaxed energy on individual tests is required.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Smooth-test and intrinsic smooth-gradient constants agree]
  \label{thm:legacy-canonical-constant-comparison}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.SmoothTest.constant_eq_intrinsic,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.SmoothTest.constant_eq_ambient,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.SmoothTest.holds_iff_intrinsic}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:legacy-relaxed-holds-equivalence,
        cor:ambient-intrinsic-constant-equality,def:selected-canonical-frontend}
  For every finite-dimensional Borel probability $\mu$,
  \[
    \CP(\mu)=\CPCan(\mu)
  \]
  as extended nonnegative constants, including the value $+\infty$. Both also equal the ambient
  relaxed optimum; the legacy and selected finite-constant predicates agree for every $C\ge0$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
   The preceding theorem identifies the sets of valid finite constants for the legacy and
  ambient-relaxed presentations. The relaxed optimizer order law turns these into finite upper
  bounds of an extended constant. If it is finite, its own nonnegative-real value is an allowed
  upper bound; if it is infinite, the set is empty. Their extended optima therefore agree.  The exact
  ambient/intrinsic equality identifies the latter with the selected constant.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Smooth and locally Lipschitz test constants agree]
  \label{thm:smooth-lipschitz-constant-equivalence}
  \lean{Solutions.ThmSmoothLipschitzConstantEquivalence.certificate}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:affine-support-intrinsic-law,def:literature-poincare-constant,
        def:legacy-smooth-test-poincare,lem:covariance-test-core-density}
  Let $\nu$ be the intrinsic law of a finite-dimensional Borel probability $\mu$.  If $\nu$ is
  absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure on its intrinsic space, then
  \[
    \CP(\mu)=\CP(\nu)=\CPLit(\mu).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Restriction to the affine support and extension constant in the normal directions give
  the first equality. Every smooth admissible test is locally Lipschitz. Conversely,
  \cref{lem:covariance-test-core-density} with $B=I$ approximates each finite-energy locally
  Lipschitz test in both $L^2$ and gradient $L^2$ by compactly supported smooth tests.
  Variance and energy converge, so each finite smooth-test constant also works on the
  locally Lipschitz domain. The sets of admissible finite constants, and hence their extended
  infima, agree. No raw/core identity for singular ambient laws is used.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Intrinsic smooth-gradient and local-slope constants agree]
  \label{thm:selected-literature-constant-equivalence}
  \lean{Solutions.ThmSelectedLiteratureConstantEquivalence.certificate}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:log-concave-law,lem:logconcave-intrinsic-absolute-continuity,
        thm:legacy-canonical-constant-comparison,thm:smooth-lipschitz-constant-equivalence}
  For every finite-dimensional log-concave probability law $\mu$,
  \[
    \CPCan(\mu)=\CPLit(\mu).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The intrinsic law is absolutely continuous by
  \cref{lem:logconcave-intrinsic-absolute-continuity}. Both sides equal the smooth-test
  optimum by the two preceding comparison
  theorems.  The Cheeger-energy identification of
  \cref{thm:smooth-gradient-relaxation-two-cheeger} is not needed.
\end{proof}

\section{Isoperimetric, affine, and one-dimensional endpoints}

\begin{definition}[Isoperimetric Cheeger constant and inverse scale]
  \label{def:isoperimetric-cheeger-scale}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isoperimetry.carrierMeasure,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isoperimetry.boundaryContent,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isoperimetry.constant,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isoperimetry.inverseScale}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:affine-support-intrinsic-law}
  Let $\mu$ be a probability law on a finite-dimensional Euclidean space $E$, and let $A$ be its
  affine support, equipped with the distance induced from $E$.  For $x\in A$ and a nonempty Borel
  set $B\subseteq A$, put
  \[
    d_A(x,B)=\inf_{y\in B}\norm{x-y},
    \qquad
    B^r=\{x\in A:d_A(x,B)<r\},
    \qquad
    \mu^+(B)=\liminf_{r\downarrow0}\frac{\mu(B^r)-\mu(B)}{r}.
  \]
  If $A$ has positive dimension, define the extended nonnegative quantities
  \[
    h_\mu=\inf_{\substack{B\subseteq A\ \mathrm{Borel}\\0<\mu(B)<1}}
      \frac{\mu^+(B)}{\min\{\mu(B),1-\mu(B)\}},
    \qquad
    \PsiKLS(\mu)=h_\mu^{-1},
  \]
  with $0^{-1}=+\infty$ and $(+\infty)^{-1}=0$.  If $A$ is zero-dimensional, set
  $h_\mu=+\infty$ and $\PsiKLS(\mu)=0$.  The isoperimetric number $h_\mu$ is not the metric energy
  $\Ch_\mu$ of \cref{thm:smooth-gradient-relaxation-two-cheeger}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Identification with the density Cheeger frontend]
  \label{thm:klartag-density-frontend-identification}
  \uses{def:log-concave-law,thm:borell-logconcave-density,
        def:affine-support-intrinsic-law,def:literature-poincare-constant,
        def:isoperimetric-cheeger-scale,lem:relative-perimeter-cheeger-infimum}
  \lean{Solutions.LemRelativePerimeterCheegerInfimum.intrinsic,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.LocalSlope.Intrinsic.constant_eq_iInf_gradient_of_logConcave}
  \leanok
  Let $\mu$ be a non-Dirac finite-dimensional log-concave probability law, let $A=a+V$ be its
  affine support, and let $\nu$ be its intrinsic law on the positive-dimensional Euclidean space
  $V$.  Let $\rho$ be the upper-semicontinuous log-concave density supplied by
  \cref{thm:borell-logconcave-density}, put $\Omega=\operatorname{int}_V K_\nu$, and define
  \[
    h_\nu^{\mathrm{sm,rel}}
      =\inf_{\substack{U\subseteq V\ \text{smooth domain}\\
                       0<\nu(U)<1}}
        \frac{\displaystyle\int_{\partial_VU\cap\Omega}\rho
          \dd\mathcal H_V^{\dim V-1}}
             {\min\{\nu(U),1-\nu(U)\}},
    \qquad
    \psi_\nu=(h_\nu^{\mathrm{sm,rel}})^{-1},
  \]
  where smooth domains locally lie on one side of their $C^\infty$ boundary, and
  $\mathcal H_V^{\dim V-1}$ is Hausdorff measure for the Euclidean metric on $V$.
  Then
  \[
    h_\mu=h_\nu^{\mathrm{sm,rel}},
    \qquad
    \PsiKLS(\mu)=\psi_\nu,
  \]
  and $\CPLit(\mu)$ equals the infimum of finite $C\ge0$ for which
  \[
    \Var_\nu(g)\le C\int_V\norm{\nabla g}^2\dd\nu
  \]
  holds for every locally Lipschitz $g\in L^2(\nu)$ with
  $\int_V\norm{\nabla g}^2\dd\nu<\infty$, with $\inf\varnothing=+\infty$.  These quantities
  do not depend on the affine origin $a$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Translation to intrinsic coordinates is an isometry. Rademacher differentiability and
  absolute continuity identify the two descriptions of the literature test energy.
  The full proof of the Cheeger equality is \cref{lem:relative-perimeter-cheeger-infimum}:
  distance cutoffs and smooth coarea give one inequality, while outward dilation about an
  interior point followed by convolution gives the other. The latter uses an explicit
  log-concave density bound to avoid a charge on the carrier boundary. The boundary of $U$
  may meet or cross $\partial K_\nu$; no condition $\partial U\subseteq\Omega$ is imposed.
  This proves equality of the two infima, without a testwise perimeter identity. The analytic
  proof is supplied in the appendix. The two isoperimetric clauses $h_\mu=h_\nu^{\mathrm{sm,rel}}$
  and $\PsiKLS(\mu)=\psi_\nu$ are Lean-certified through
  \cref{lem:relative-perimeter-cheeger-infimum}
  (\texttt{Solutions.LemRelativePerimeterCheegerInfimum.intrinsic}).  The
  $\CPLit(\mu)$ clause is a local Lean proof as well
  (a generic local-slope comparison and its log-concave specialization): $\CPLit$ is defined through the local
  metric slope, and Rademacher differentiability together with the intrinsic absolute continuity of
  every log-concave law identifies the slope with $\norm{\nabla g}$ almost everywhere, hence the
  admissible class and the energy, hence the two infima.  The reference Haar measure is
  constructed inside the proof rather than assumed, so the statement carries neither a reference
  measure nor an affine origin, which is the last sentence of the theorem.  All three clauses being
  local Lean proofs.  The separate
  Cheeger--Buser comparison below is also a local Lean proof;
  neither gives the dimension-free KLS bound by itself.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Cheeger--Buser comparison for log-concave laws]
  \label{thm:logconcave-cheeger-poincare-comparison}
  \uses{def:log-concave-law,def:isoperimetric-cheeger-scale,
        def:literature-poincare-constant,thm:klartag-density-frontend-identification,
        lem:covariance-test-core-density}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.literatureConstant_le_four_mul_inverseScale_sq,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isoperimetry.smoothTest_constant_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.inverseScale_sq_le_pi_literature}
  \leanok
  Every finite-dimensional log-concave probability law satisfies
  \[
    \CPLit(\mu)\le4\PsiKLS(\mu)^2,
    \qquad
    \PsiKLS(\mu)^2\le\pi\CPLit(\mu).
  \]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  If $\mu$ is a Dirac law, then its affine support is a singleton, $\PsiKLS(\mu)=0$, and
  $\CPLit(\mu)=0$ because every test is $\mu$-almost everywhere constant.  Thus both inequalities
  hold directly.  Otherwise apply \cref{thm:klartag-density-frontend-identification}.  Cheeger's
  inequality in the normalization of Milman's Theorem~1.1 and Bobkov's equation~(1.3) gives the
  first inequality; this first inequality is also a local Lean proof
  (a median split, the layer-cake
  formula, the multiplied Minkowski infimum bound and the Minkowski coarea inequality give
  $h_M^2\int(g-m)_+^2\le4\int_{\{g>m\}}\norm{\nabla g}^2$ for bounded $C^1$ Lipschitz $g$, a
  smooth cutoff extends this to every smooth test, and \cref{lem:relative-perimeter-cheeger-infimum}
  transports it to $\CPLit(\mu)\le4\PsiKLS(\mu)^2$). The second inequality is also a
  local Lean proof. The intrinsic Borell law is convolved with the normalized
  Gaussian kernel. Its positive smooth convex potential gives the exact smooth-model $\pi$ bound;
  its literature Poincaré constant is at most the source constant plus the Gaussian variance.
  A finite lower bound on the approximants' Minkowski Cheeger constants passes through the
  smooth Lipschitz test inequality under setwise convergence of laws, using weak convergence
  only for bounded continuous gradients. The resulting source-law bound is optimized over
  finite constants and transported through intrinsic coordinates. This includes zero-dimensional
  affine support and zero or infinite optimal constants. The argument does not assert
  convergence of the relative perimeter of each individual domain.
  Klartag's equation~(1.4), with the right-hand bound due to
  De Ponti--Mondino, gives the second; see
  \cite{Milman2009Isoperimetric,Bobkov1999LogConcave,
        Klartag2023Logarithmic,DePontiMondino2021}.
  To make the boundary convention explicit, De Ponti--Mondino define their Cheeger constant
  using metric BV perimeter. Work on the closed intrinsic support $K=K_\nu$, with its induced
  distance and probability measure $\nu$, and write
  \[
    h_{\mathrm{BV}}(K,\nu)
      =\inf_{\substack{B\subseteq K\ \mathrm{Borel}\\0<\nu(B)<1}}
        \frac{\operatorname{Per}_\nu(B)}{\min\{\nu(B),1-\nu(B)\}}.
  \]
  Symmetry of BV perimeter under complementation identifies this with their convention
  $0<\nu(B)\le1/2$. By Lemma~2.1 and Theorem~3.6 of
  \cite{AmbrosioDiMarinoGigli2017Minkowski}, perimeter is the $L^1$ relaxation of outer
  Minkowski content. Hence the corresponding Cheeger infima agree: perimeter is bounded by
  Minkowski content, while recovery sets and continuity of
  $\min\{\nu(B),1-\nu(B)\}$ under $L^1$ convergence give the reverse inequality.
  Restricting a set to $K$ preserves its mass and can only decrease its measured enlargement;
  for sets already in $K$, intrinsic enlargements have the same measure as ambient ones.
  Thus $h_\mu=h_{\mathrm{BV}}(K,\nu)$. This is an equality of infima, not a claim that each
  set's perimeter equals its unrelaxed Minkowski content.

  De Ponti--Mondino's equation~(2) uses the Rayleigh numerator $\int|\nabla f|^2\,d\nu$,
  without the factor $1/2$ in their Cheeger energy. Its tests are nonzero, mean-zero functions
  in $\operatorname{Lip}_b(K)$, meaning bounded Lipschitz functions with bounded support.
  To identify this test class with the literature one, apply
  \cref{lem:covariance-test-core-density} with $B=I$ to a nonzero centered literature test
  $g$, obtaining $g_j\in C_c^\infty(V)$ converging to $g$ in both $L^2(\nu)$ and gradient
  $L^2(\nu;V)$. Fix $\psi\in C_c^\infty(V)$ with $\int\psi\,d\nu=1$ and put
  \[
    u_j=g_j-\Bigl(\int g_j\,d\nu\Bigr)\psi.
  \]
  Since $\int g_j\,d\nu\to0$, the same two convergences hold for $u_j$; its restriction to
  $K$ has zero mean and bounded support, and is nonzero in $L^2$ for all sufficiently large
  $j$. The metric slope on $K$ equals the ambient gradient norm $\nu$-almost everywhere,
  since $\nu(\partial K)=0$. Conversely, a source test has a bounded Lipschitz extension
  to $V$, with the same values and gradient norm $\nu$-almost everywhere. Passing to
  Rayleigh quotients therefore gives $\lambda_1^{-1}=\CPLit(\mu)$, with the extended
  reciprocal conventions. The zero-dimensional case was handled separately above.

  Their Corollary~1.2, equation~(12), gives
  a coefficient strictly smaller than $\pi$ in the zero-curvature case.
  We retain the convenient bound $\pi$ in Klartag's formula and make no optimality
  claim for it. Their sharp Buser estimate concerns the positive-curvature case.
  The numerical inequalities above are local Lean proofs. The metric BV test class
  is the bounded-support \texttt{Lip\_b} class used by De Ponti--Mondino; the
  sequential perimeter definition, closed-support law, finite-space complement
  symmetry, and zero-dimensional half-mass test convention are formalized in Lean.
  Complement symmetry also holds for every finite measure on every metric
  space, in particular for the closed-support law on an arbitrary unbounded support:
  the reflection of a test is clamped to $[0,1]$ and cut off radially, and the
  errors are masses outside large balls. Finiteness is necessary, since for Lebesgue measure on
  $\mathbb R$ the empty set has perimeter zero and the line has infinite perimeter. For a
  finite measure, globally Lipschitz approximants with arbitrary constants, values and supports,
  converging to the indicator in the global $L^1(\nu)$ distance, may replace bounded-support
  tests: clamping to $[0,1]$ and a radial cutoff give tests with the same limit and no larger
  liminf cost, so the perimeter is at most that liminf. These are the approximants
  of Definition~3.3 of \cite{AmbrosioDiMarinoGigli2017Minkowski} (globally Lipschitz, global
  $L^1$ convergence). On a proper metric space the same bound holds for locally Lipschitz
  approximants with global $L^1$ convergence, a variant discussed in their Remark~3.5
  (not the article's working definition, which is the global $L^1$ class of Definition~3.3): the
  truncation is carried by a compact closed ball, on which a locally Lipschitz function is
  Lipschitz. For every finite Borel measure the relaxation infimum over the approximants of
  Definition~3.3 equals the perimeter used here, and on a proper space so does the infimum over
  locally Lipschitz approximants with global $L^1$ convergence, in particular on the closed
  intrinsic support of every finite-dimensional law. On a proper space, locally Lipschitz approximants
  converging in $L^1$ on every closed ball (equivalently on every compact set) also bound the
  perimeter after clamping, and the infimum over such approximants equals the perimeter
  as well; the $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ variant beyond proper spaces
  and finite measures is not a local Lean theorem. The equality of the half-mass and symmetric BV Cheeger constants
  holds for every probability measure on a metric space, by replacing a test set
  of mass above one half with its complement. For this bounded-support sequential relaxation the
  BV--Minkowski Cheeger-infimum equality is a local Lean proof for every probability
  law on a normed space: perimeter is at most outer Minkowski content, and measurable superlevel
  sets of recovery tests, whose masses, contents and quotients converge, give the reverse
  inequality; hence the symmetric and half-mass BV constants of the intrinsic coordinate law equal
  $h_\mu$. This is an equality of infima. On the closed intrinsic support $K$ itself, when the
  intrinsic law has convex support, the nearest-point projection onto $K$ is a nonexpansive
  retraction, so each set and its trace on $K$ have equal perimeter and mass; for every
  finite-dimensional log-concave law this gives $h_{\mathrm{BV}}(K,\nu)=h_\mu$ as a local Lean
  proof, for the bounded-support relaxation used here. The relaxed lower Minkowski
  content, the infimum of the liminf of outer Minkowski contents over measurable sets converging
  in $L^1$, equals the perimeter used here for every probability law on a normed space;
  this is an equality of infima, and the pointwise inequality between perimeter and
  outer content can be strict. The upper Minkowski content, the $\limsup$ of the same radius
  quotient, is at least the lower content; for every set the real mass of its open
  $s$-thickening is monotone in $s$, hence differentiable at Lebesgue-almost every radius, and at
  every radius where it has a derivative $d$ the upper content of that thickening is at most $d$
  (for probability laws on normed groups): the $t$-thickening of the $s$-thickening lies
  in the $(s+t)$-thickening, so each content quotient is bounded by the right difference quotient
  of the radius mass. No absolute continuity of the radius mass is asserted (jumps and singular
  variation remain possible). Integrating over the radii, for every set $B$ and every $r>0$ the
  upper content of the $s$-thickening integrated over $s\in(0,r)$ is at most $\nu(B^r)-\nu(B)$:
  almost everywhere the derivative is the density of the Lebesgue--Stieltjes
  measure of the right-continuous modification of the radius mass, whose integral over $(0,r)$
  is at most that measure's mass of $(0,r)$, and one-sided limits at the endpoints bound this
  mass by the increment; only inequalities are used, with no measurability of the content, no
  integrability of the derivative and no absolute continuity. From the integrated bound, a
  quantitative selection of a radius $s\in(0,r)$ with upper content of $B^s$ at most the
  lower-content quotient at $r$ plus any slack recovers every measurable set of finite lower
  content by thickenings converging in $L^1$ with $\limsup$ of upper contents at most the lower
  content (the vanishing increments follow from the finite lower content, without
  $\nu(\overline B)=\nu(B)$); every set of finite BV perimeter is recovered by measurable sets
  converging in $L^1$ whose upper contents tend to the perimeter, through the lower recovery sets
  and BV lower semicontinuity below the upper content; and the relaxed upper Minkowski content,
  the infimum of $\liminf$ upper contents over the same measurable approximating class, equals
  the perimeter used here for every set, hence the relaxed lower content (equalities
  of infima; the pointwise upper content is not identified with the perimeter).
  This content chain (both Minkowski contents, the local-slope Minkowski coarea inequality, the
  bridge, both recoveries and both relaxed identities) is a local Lean result on an arbitrary
  metric space with a Borel probability law: the generic coarea inequality uses the supremum over
  open balls of \cite[(3.1)]{AmbrosioDiMarinoGigli2017Minkowski}, which is lower semicontinuous on
  every metric space, whereas the closed-ball supremum need not
  be continuous there. The chain holds for every finite Borel
  measure on a metric space: the contents, recoveries and relaxations are defined for a finite
  measure (probability laws are instances), costs, distances, approximating classes, perimeter and
  both contents scale by nonnegative real scalars, the zero measure and the normalization of a
  finite nonzero measure are explicit, and the lower and upper recoveries and both relaxed
  identities hold for arbitrary targets under finite total mass. In the article the
  bounded-support reduction is Remark~3.4, Definition~3.3 uses global $L^1$ convergence, and
  Remark~3.5 discusses alternate local-convergence definitions that are not its working
  definition; its Theorem~3.6 concerns Borel sets of finite measure under Borel measures finite on
  bounded sets; both relaxed-content identities and the recoveries are proved at
  that generality, and so is the full coarea radius-liminf statement.
  A supporting result establishes Remark~3.4's
  bounded-support reduction for every measurable finite-mass target under any Borel measure on
  a metric space: cutoff error and additional slope cost are bounded by the original global
  indicator $L^1$ error and target mass outside expanding balls. Fixed radii give
  bounded-support approximants, and the globally Lipschitz relaxation of Definition~3.3 equals
  the bounded-support perimeter, without finite total mass or properness.
  Further supporting results preserve lower content under restriction whenever a positive
  thickening lies in the restriction region, prove the direct positive-superlevel coarea upper
  bound under finite mass of a positive-support neighborhood (the all-level bound for
  nonnegative functions), and prove perimeter at most lower content at measurable finite-mass
  targets under any Borel measure. Under finiteness on bounded Borel sets, quantitative
  selection gives bounded measurable lower-recovery sets of finite mass and
  finite lower content, converging in global indicator $L^1$ with lower contents converging
  to target perimeter. The relaxed lower-content infimum equals perimeter at
  measurable finite-mass targets, including infinite perimeter; a finite prefix may carry
  infinite mass because convergence gives eventual finite approximating-set mass.
  Finite target mass and finite lower content also imply a finite-mass positive
  neighborhood; restriction to it and a tail of small radii transfer genuine upper
  contents and original global indicator $L^1$ convergence, proving the lower-to-upper
  recovery of Lemma~2.1 at finite-mass Borel targets. A diagonal choice gives bounded
  open finite-mass upper recovery with finite upper content term by term and upper
  contents converging to finite target perimeter. The relaxed upper-content
  infimum equals perimeter, hence the lower relaxation, at Theorem~3.6's scope,
  including infinite perimeter by order and allowing infinite-mass finite prefixes.
  The full Lemma~3.2 chain is also formalized with its right-radius
  $\liminf$ of integrated open-ball growth between the positive-level content
  integral and the original local-slope integral. Nonnegativity makes growth vanish
  outside the positive-support neighborhood at small radii, so restriction to that
  finite region preserves the middle quantity. No function integrability
  hypothesis is added. Lemma~2.1's relaxed-upper-content bound by lower content is
  proved for measurable finite-mass targets under any Borel measure, using
  radius recovery in the finite-content branch and order at infinity.
  Lemma~2.1's equality of the relaxed lower and upper contents is also proved for every
  Borel measure at measurable finite-mass targets, by near-minimum selection
  and global-$L^1$ lower semicontinuity of the relaxed upper content; this equality of infima
  identifies neither relaxation with the perimeter beyond the scopes above. On a proper
  metric space, Remark~3.5's locally Lipschitz classes with global $L^1$ convergence and with
  convergence on every compact set have relaxation infima equal to the bounded-support
  perimeter at measurable finite-mass targets for every Borel measure: sharper
  truncation estimates replace mass outside a ball by the indicator error on a closed ball and
  the target outside it, and radii are chosen diagonally. Nonconvex ambient/support transfer,
  the locally Lipschitz classes on nonproper spaces and general metric-measure comparisons
  are not formalized.
  The Lean proof of the numerical inequalities uses the proved intrinsic Minkowski and
  relative-perimeter infimum equality instead.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Letwin's ambient Poincar\'e constant (1.1)]
  \label{lem:letwin-ambient-poincare-constant}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.localSlope_constant_eq_ambient_gradient,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.one_le_localSlope_constant,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.LocalSlope.holds_iff_lintegral_gradient_le_one,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.LocalSlope.Holds.memLp_two}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:literature-poincare-constant}
  Letwin \cite{Letwin2026QuadraticKLS}, equation~(1.1). Let $\mu$ be a log-concave probability law on a
  finite-dimensional real inner product space, absolutely continuous with respect to an additive Haar measure. Then
  $\CPLit(\mu)$ is the infimum of the $C\ge0$ such that $\Var_\mu g\le C\int\norm{\nabla g}^2\dd\mu$ for every locally
  Lipschitz $g$ with $\int\norm{\nabla g}^2\dd\mu\le1$. Here $\nabla g$ is the almost-everywhere Fr\'echet gradient, no
  $L^2$ premise is imposed and the variance is extended-valued. If $\mu$ is moreover isotropic in positive dimension,
  then $\CPLit(\mu)\ge1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  A local-slope Poincar\'e inequality forces $L^2$ membership of every finite-energy locally Lipschitz test
  (truncation at $\pm k$ and Fatou), and the inequality is homogeneous, so unit-energy tests suffice. Rademacher's
  theorem identifies the slope with $\norm{\nabla g}$ almost everywhere. Linear tests give $\CPLit(\mu)\ge1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Letwin's smooth-boundary isoperimetric constant]
  \label{lem:letwin-smooth-boundary-isoperimetry}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isoperimetry.constant_eq_smoothConstant_univ,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isoperimetry.inverseScale_eq_inv_smoothConstant_univ,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isoperimetry.minkowskiConstant_eq_smoothConstant_univ,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isoperimetry.constant_eq_minkowskiConstant,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isoperimetry.minkowskiConstant_eq_of_isometryEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isoperimetry.smoothConstant_mono}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:isoperimetric-cheeger-scale, thm:klartag-density-frontend-identification}
  Letwin \cite{Letwin2026QuadraticKLS}, Section~1, the display defining $1/\psi_\mu$. Let $\mu$ be a probability law on
  a finite-dimensional real inner product space with a Borell (upper semicontinuous log-concave) density $\rho$. Then
  \[
    h_\mu=\inf_{A}\frac{\int_{\partial A}\rho\dd\mathcal H^{n-1}}{\min\{\mu(A),1-\mu(A)\}},
    \qquad
    \PsiKLS(\mu)=\psi_\mu,
  \]
  the infimum over open $A$ with smooth boundary and $0<\mu(A)<1$, with the boundary integral over all of $\partial A$.
  Full affine support is derived from the density, not assumed. The supremum $\psi_n$ over isotropic laws is used
  inline (Lemma~\ref{lem:letwin-kls-conjecture-forms}) and has no separate definition.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  With full affine support, $h_\mu$ is the Minkowski Cheeger constant of $\mu$ itself. The smooth-domain infimum is
  monotone in the region over which the boundary is integrated; the relative-perimeter equality of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:klartag-density-frontend-identification} and the smooth-test bound by Minkowski content give both
  inequalities. Minkowski Cheeger constants are invariant under measure-preserving isometries.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Letwin's Cheeger--Buser ratio]
  \label{thm:letwin-cheeger-buser-ratio}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.inverseScale_sq_div_literatureConstant_mem_Icc,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.inv_smoothConstant_univ_sq_div_literatureConstant_mem_Icc,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.literatureConstant_le_four_mul_inv_smoothConstant_univ_sq,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.inv_smoothConstant_univ_sq_le_pi_literature,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.localSlope_constant_pos_of_affineSupport_eq_top,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.localSlope_constant_lt_top_of_conjecture}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:logconcave-cheeger-poincare-comparison, lem:letwin-ambient-poincare-constant,
        lem:letwin-smooth-boundary-isoperimetry, conj:kls}
  Letwin \cite{Letwin2026QuadraticKLS}, Section~1, the unnumbered display quoting Cheeger, Buser--Ledoux and De
  Ponti--Mondino. For $n\ge1$ and every log-concave probability law $\mu$ on $\R^n$ with a Borell density,
  $0<\CPLit(\mu)<\infty$ and
  \[
    \frac14\le\frac{\psi_\mu^2}{\CPLit(\mu)}\le\pi .
  \]
  The two inequalities $\CPLit(\mu)\le4\psi_\mu^2$ and $\psi_\mu^2\le\pi\,\CPLit(\mu)$ hold in every dimension without
  the KLS bound. Finiteness of $\CPLit(\mu)$, needed for the ratio, comes from Conjecture~\ref{conj:kls} (proved); the
  family form takes the conjecture as a hypothesis.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Combine Theorem~\ref{thm:logconcave-cheeger-poincare-comparison} with
  Lemma~\ref{lem:letwin-smooth-boundary-isoperimetry}. Full affine support gives $\CPLit(\mu)>0$, and
  $\CPLit(\mu)\le C\norm{\Cov_\mu}_{\op}$ gives $\CPLit(\mu)<\infty$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Isotropic and $\psi_n$ forms of the KLS conjecture]
  \label{lem:letwin-kls-conjecture-forms}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.conjecture_iff_isotropic,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.conjecture_iff_inverseScale,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.localSlope_constant_le_four_of_isotropic_fin_one}
  \leanok
  \uses{conj:kls, thm:affine-isotropic-reduction, thm:logconcave-cheeger-poincare-comparison,
        thm:one-dimensional-logconcave-poincare}
  Letwin \cite{Letwin2026QuadraticKLS}, Section~1, statement of the KLS conjecture. Conjecture~\ref{conj:kls} holds if
  and only if one constant bounds $\CPLit(\mu)$ for every isotropic log-concave $\mu$ on $\R^n$ in every dimension, and
  if and only if $\sup_{n\ge2}\psi_n<\infty$, where $\psi_n$ is the supremum of $\PsiKLS(\mu)$ over isotropic
  log-concave $\mu$ on $\R^n$. Neither equivalence uses the proof of the conjecture. In dimension one every isotropic
  log-concave $\mu$ has $\CPLit(\mu)\le4$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The affine/isotropic reduction gives the first equivalence. For the second, the Cheeger--Buser comparison transfers
  bounds between $\CPLit$ and $\PsiKLS^2$ when $n\ge2$; dimension one uses the one-dimensional bound $4\Var$ and
  dimension zero has $\CPLit=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Perimeter as relaxed Minkowski content]
  \label{thm:relaxed-minkowski-content-perimeter}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.MetricBV.relaxedMinkowskiContent_eq_perimeter_of_finite_on_bounded,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.MetricBV.lipschitzRelaxation_eq_perimeter_of_measure_lt_top,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.MetricBV.relaxedUpperMinkowskiContent_eq_perimeter_of_finite_on_bounded,
        Solutions.ThmRelaxedMinkowskiContentPerimeter.certificate}
  \leanok
  This is Theorem~3.6 of \cite{AmbrosioDiMarinoGigli2017Minkowski} (arXiv:1603.08412v1), with
  the perimeter of its Definition~3.3.  Let $(X,\mathsf d)$ be a metric space and $\mathfrak m$ a
  Borel measure that is finite on bounded Borel sets.  For a Borel set $A$ with
  $\mathfrak m(A)<\infty$ let
  \[
    \mathcal M_-(A)=\liminf_{r\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathfrak m(A^r)-\mathfrak m(A)}{r},\qquad
    A^r=\{x:\mathsf d_A(x)<r\},
  \]
  and define the relaxed lower Minkowski content and the perimeter by
  \[
    \mathcal M_-^\ast(A)=\inf\Bigl\{\liminf_{h\to\infty}\mathcal M_-(A_h):
      \mathfrak m(A_h\,\triangle\,A)\to 0\Bigr\},\qquad
    \operatorname{Per}(A)=\inf\Bigl\{\liminf_{h\to\infty}\int_X|\nabla f_h|\,d\mathfrak m:
      f_h\in\operatorname{Lip}(X),\ \int_X|f_h-\chi_A|\,d\mathfrak m\to 0\Bigr\},
  \]
  with Borel approximating sets, $|\nabla f|$ the local slope (zero at isolated points) and
  $\inf\emptyset=\infty$.  Then $\mathcal M_-^\ast(A)=\operatorname{Per}(A)$.  The same holds for
  the relaxed upper content, defined with $\limsup$; together these give the second clause of
  Lemma~2.1 of the paper.  These are equalities of infima, not identities between the content
  and the perimeter of an individual set.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The relaxed lower and upper contents both equal the bounded-support metric BV perimeter at
  measurable finite-mass targets, under the finiteness of $\mathfrak m$ on bounded Borel sets:
  perimeter is at most the lower content, perimeter is lower semicontinuous under indicator
  $L^1$ convergence, and actual recovery sets have finite mass and contents converging to the
  perimeter.  The truncation and bounded-support reduction of Remark~3.4 identifies the
  Definition~3.3 relaxation with the same perimeter at finite-mass targets, for every Borel
  measure.  The certificate composes the two identities.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Isotropic probability law]
  \label{def:isotropic-law}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isotropic}
  \leanok
  A probability law $\nu$ with finite second moment on a finite-dimensional Euclidean space $V$
  is isotropic when
  \[
    \int_V x\dd\nu(x)=0,
    \qquad
    \Cov_\nu=I_V.
  \]
  The unique probability law on the zero-dimensional space is included.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Ambient and intrinsic covariance agree]
  \label{lem:ambient-intrinsic-covariance}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Covariance.operator_apply_eq_zero_of_mem_orthogonal,
        FunctionalInequalities.Covariance.bilin_intrinsic,
        FunctionalInequalities.Covariance.operator_coe_intrinsic,
        FunctionalInequalities.Covariance.opNorm_operator_intrinsic,
        FunctionalInequalities.Covariance.intrinsic_operator_inner_self_pos,
        FunctionalInequalities.Covariance.operator_eq_zero_of_direction_eq_bot,
        Solutions.LemAmbientIntrinsicCovariance.certificate}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:affine-support-intrinsic-law}
  Let $\mu$ be a Borel probability law with finite second moment on a finite-dimensional Euclidean
  space $E$, and let $A=a+V$ be its affine support.  Let $\Cov_{\mu,V}$ be the covariance operator
  of its intrinsic law on $V$.  The ambient covariance $\Cov_\mu$ annihilates $V^\perp$, restricts
  to $\Cov_{\mu,V}$ on $V$, and satisfies
  \[
    \norm{\Cov_\mu}_\op=\norm{\Cov_{\mu,V}}_\op.
  \]
  Moreover, $\Cov_{\mu,V}$ is positive definite on $V$; in dimension zero both covariance
  operators and both norms are zero.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The barycenter lies in $A$, because $A$ is closed and convex and carries $\mu$, so the centered
  random vector lies in $V$ almost surely.  Hence the covariance form vanishes whenever one
  argument is in $V^\perp$, and the operator annihilates $V^\perp$.  The intrinsic law is the
  image of $\mu$ under translation by the chosen support point followed by orthogonal projection
  onto $V$; translation leaves the covariance form unchanged and the adjoint of the projection is
  the inclusion, so the intrinsic form is the restriction of the ambient form.  Decomposing an
  ambient vector as its projection plus an orthogonal remainder gives the restriction identity for
  the operators and, with the norm bound of the projection, the equality of operator norms.  If the
  intrinsic form vanished on a nonzero $v\in V$, the variance of $\langle v,\cdot\rangle$ would be
  zero, so $\nu$ would be carried by the proper affine hyperplane on which $\langle v,\cdot\rangle$
  equals its mean, contradicting the fact that the affine support of the intrinsic law is all of
  $V$.  In dimension zero $V^\perp=E$, so both operators are zero.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Affine covariance relaxation and Poincar\'e constant]
  \label{def:affine-poincare-constant}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Energy.CovarianceGradient.density,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.CovarianceGradient.Test,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.CovarianceGradient.preEnergy,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.CovarianceGradient.relaxation,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.CovarianceGradient.Intrinsic.preEnergy,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.CovarianceGradient.Intrinsic.energy,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.CovarianceGradient.Intrinsic.constant,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.CovarianceGradient.Intrinsic.Holds,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.CovarianceGradient.Intrinsic.constant_eq_zero_of_direction_eq_bot,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.intrinsic_memLp_id_two,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.intrinsic_ae_hasGradientAt,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.intrinsicCovariance_energy_eq_of_toL2_eq}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:log-concave-law,thm:borell-logconcave-density,
        def:affine-support-intrinsic-law,def:preenergy-relaxation,
        def:l2-energy-poincare-constant,lem:ambient-intrinsic-covariance}
  Let $\mu$ be a log-concave probability law on a finite-dimensional Euclidean space, let $\nu$ be
  its intrinsic law on the direction space $V$ of its affine support, and put
  $\Sigma=\Cov_{\mu,V}=\Cov_\nu$.
  Let $P^{\mathrm{aff}}_\mu$ be the pre-energy whose tests are globally Lipschitz functions
  $g\in L^2(\nu)$ with
  \[
    \int_V\inner{\Sigma\nabla g}{\nabla g}\dd\nu<\infty,
  \]
  whose realization is their $L^2(\nu)$ class, and whose finite energy is the displayed integral.
  The absolute continuity in \cref{thm:borell-logconcave-density} and Rademacher's theorem make the
  gradient well defined $\nu$-almost everywhere.  Define
  \[
    \mathcal E^{\mathrm{aff}}_\mu
      :=(P^{\mathrm{aff}}_\mu).\mathrm{sequentialRelaxation},
    \qquad
    C_{\mathrm P}^{\mathrm{aff}}(\mu)
      :=\CE\bigl(\nu,\mathcal E^{\mathrm{aff}}_\mu\bigr).
  \]
  Thus the closure of the covariance pre-form means its sequential $L^2(\nu)$ relaxation.  In
  dimension zero the resulting constant is zero.
  The Lean definition is implemented for every Borel probability law on a finite-dimensional
  Euclidean space, with $\Sigma$ the covariance operator of $\nu$ in Mathlib's convention and
  $\nabla g$ the canonical Hilbert gradient, zero where $g$ is not differentiable; the definition
  itself therefore needs no differentiability premise.  For log-concave $\mu$ the three
  well-definedness contracts of the displayed sentence are proved: $\nu$ has a finite second
  moment, so $\Sigma$ is the honest covariance; every test is differentiable $\nu$-almost
  everywhere with the canonical gradient as derivative; and continuous representatives of one
  $L^2(\nu)$ class have $\nu$-almost everywhere equal gradients, so the energy depends only on
  the class.  This is a definition node: closability, agreement with the raw energy, translation
  invariance and every Poincar\'e statement are separate nodes.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The affine covariance relaxation agrees with the closed source form]
  \label{thm:affine-covariance-relaxation-closed}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.exists_norm_sq_eq_inner_of_isSymmetric,
        FunctionalInequalities.Covariance.exists_inner_eq_norm_sq,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.PreEnergy.normSqEnergy,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.PreEnergy.sequentialRelaxation_ofLinearPMap_eq_normSqEnergy,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.translateLaw,
        FunctionalInequalities.L2.Translation.pullbackEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.L2.Translation.variance_pullbackEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.Covariance.operator_translateLaw,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.CovarianceGradient.relaxation_translateLaw,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.WeightedGradient.constantSmoothCore,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.intrinsicCovariance_denseRealization,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.intrinsicCovariance_closedQuadratic,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.intrinsicCovariance_isSequentialCoreFor,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.CovarianceGradient.Intrinsic.constant_eq_translate_intrinsicMeasure,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.intrinsicCovariance_energy_eq_normSq,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.intrinsicCovariance_constant_eq_normSq,
        Solutions.ThmAffineCovarianceRelaxationClosed.certificate}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:affine-poincare-constant,thm:borell-logconcave-density,
        lem:ambient-intrinsic-covariance,thm:quadratic-relaxation-closed,
        lem:covariance-core-closure}
  For every log-concave law on a finite-dimensional Euclidean space, $P^{\mathrm{aff}}_\mu$ is
  quadratic with dense realization and is closable.  Its sequential relaxation is a closed
  quadratic form and agrees with the raw covariance energy on every globally Lipschitz test.
  Changing the affine origin translates $\nu$; pullback by this translation is an $L^2$ isometry
  preserving variance and covariance energy.  Under this isometric identification,
  $\mathcal E^{\mathrm{aff}}_\mu$ is the closed covariance form used by the pinned CMH source, and
  $C_{\mathrm P}^{\mathrm{aff}}(\mu)$ is the same optimal constant.
  In Lean, quadraticity of $P^{\mathrm{aff}}_\mu$ is expressed through a norm factor $B$ for the
  intrinsic covariance, $\inner{\Sigma v}{v}=\norm{Bv}^2$: the pre-energy has the same
  realization and energies as the quadratic pre-energy $f\mapsto\norm{B\nabla f}^2$ of the
  closable operator of \cref{lem:covariance-core-closure}.  The translation statement is proved
  for every translate of $\nu$ by a vector of $V$ and for every Borel law, and the closed covariance
  form is the squared-norm energy of the closure of $B\nabla$ restricted to the classes of
  $\mathbb R+C_c^\infty(V)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Apply \cref{lem:covariance-core-closure} to the intrinsic law and its covariance.
  That lemma proves closability by distributional integration by parts on compact subsets
  of the support interior, identifies both graph closures by actual cutoff and convolution
  approximations, and proves that the graph-closed energy equals the sequential relaxation.
  Thus raw energy is preserved on every original test. Translation pullback preserves the
  covariance matrix, gradient, measure and variance, so it also preserves both the graph norm
  and its optimal Poincar\'e constant. Dimension zero has only constant classes and zero energy.
  The pinned source, \texttt{modules/kls/41-cmh-normalization.tex}, defines its covariance
  domain by completing the restrictions to $K_\nu$ of $\mathbb R+C_c^\infty(V)$ in the norm
  \[
    \left(\|f\|_{L^2(\nu)}^2+
      \int\langle\Sigma\nabla f,\nabla f\rangle\,d\nu\right)^{1/2}.
  \]
  These are restrictions of ambient compactly supported functions, not functions compactly
  supported inside $\Omega$. Consequently their closure imposes no zero boundary trace.
  The preceding approximation and closability proof identifies exactly this source domain.
  The Lean certificate packages dense realization, the closed quadratic form, the sequential
  core, the translation isometry with its variance, energy and constant identities, and the
  identification with the squared-norm energy of the closure on the core $\mathbb R+C_c^\infty(V)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Translation invariance of the affine Poincar\'e constant]
  \label{lem:affine-poincare-translation-invariance}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Energy.Euclidean.gradient_comp_linearIsometryEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.Covariance.operator_map_linearIsometryEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.mapLinearIsometryEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.L2.LinearIsometry.pullbackEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.L2.LinearIsometry.variance_pullbackEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.CovarianceGradient.relaxation_mapLinearIsometryEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.CovarianceGradient.constant_mapLinearIsometryEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.affineSupport_translateLaw,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.direction_affineSupport_translateLaw,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.intrinsicMeasure_translateLaw,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.CovarianceGradient.Intrinsic.exists_translateLaw_isometry,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.CovarianceGradient.Intrinsic.constant_translateLaw,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.translateLaw,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.memLp_id_two,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.integral_id_translateLaw_neg_integral,
        Solutions.LemAffinePoincareTranslationInvariance.certificate}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:log-concave-law,def:affine-poincare-constant,
        thm:borell-logconcave-density,thm:affine-covariance-relaxation-closed}
  Let $\mu$ be a log-concave probability law on a finite-dimensional Euclidean space $E$, let
  $b\in E$, and put $\tau_b(x)=x+b$.  Then $(\tau_b)_\#\mu$ is log-concave and
  \[
    C_{\mathrm P}^{\mathrm{aff}}\bigl((\tau_b)_\#\mu\bigr)
      =C_{\mathrm P}^{\mathrm{aff}}(\mu).
  \]
  In particular, translating by the negative barycenter produces a centered law without changing
  the affine Poincar\'e constant.
  In Lean, $(\tau_b)_\#\mu$ is \texttt{Probability.translateLaw}~$\mu\,b$.  The affine
  support of the translated law is the translate of the affine support of $\mu$, with the same
  direction submodule, and the intrinsic law of the translated law is the translate, inside the
  direction space, of the image of the intrinsic law of $\mu$ under the linear isometric
  identification of the two direction submodules.  The constant identity, the invariance of the
  ambient covariance operator and of the direction, and the variance-preserving $L^2$ isometry
  intertwining the two affine relaxations are proved for every Borel probability law on $E$;
  log-concavity is used only for log-concavity of the translated law and for the ambient second
  moment that gives the barycenter.  Dimension zero is included.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Compact sets and their Minkowski combinations commute with translation, so log-concavity is
  preserved.  Translation preserves covariance and identifies the two intrinsic laws by an
  $L^2$ isometry.  The isometry preserves variance and intertwines the covariance pre-energies,
  hence also their sequential relaxations and optimal constants.  The barycenter exists by
  \cref{thm:borell-logconcave-density}.
  The Lean certificate packages log-concavity of the translated law, invariance of the ambient
  covariance operator and of the direction of the affine support, the variance-preserving $L^2$
  isometry intertwining the affine relaxations, the constant identity, the ambient second moment,
  and the zero mean of the translate by the negative barycenter.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Affine and isotropic KLS formulations]
  \label{thm:affine-isotropic-reduction}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Energy.PreEnergy.sequentialRelaxation_comp_le_mul,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.Euclidean.gradient_comp_continuousLinearEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.Covariance.operator_map_continuousLinearEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.mapContinuousLinearEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.L2.ContinuousLinearEquiv.pullbackEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.CovarianceGradient.relaxation_mapContinuousLinearEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.CovarianceGradient.constant_mapContinuousLinearEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.exists_whitening_of_isSymmetric,
        FunctionalInequalities.Covariance.exists_whitening,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.LipschitzGradient.relaxation,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.LipschitzGradient.holds_iff_localSlope,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.SmoothTest.constant_eq_lipschitz,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.CovarianceGradient.relaxation_eq_lipschitz,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.CovarianceGradient.relaxation_le_mul_lipschitz,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.of_continuousLinearEquiv_map,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.IsotropicBound,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.CovarianceBound,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.AffineBound,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.exists_isotropic_localSlope_constant_eq,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.localSlope_le_mul_intrinsicCovariance,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.affineBound_of_isotropicBound,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.covarianceBound_of_affineBound,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.isotropicBound_of_covarianceBound,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.affine_isotropic_reduction,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.conjecture_of_affineBound,
        Solutions.ThmAffineIsotropicReduction.certificate}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:log-concave-law,thm:borell-logconcave-density,
        def:isotropic-law,def:literature-poincare-constant,
        lem:ambient-intrinsic-covariance,def:affine-poincare-constant,
        thm:affine-covariance-relaxation-closed,
        lem:affine-poincare-translation-invariance}
  For a fixed $K\ge0$, the following statements are equivalent.
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item Every isotropic log-concave law $\nu$ on every finite-dimensional Euclidean space
          satisfies $\CPLit(\nu)\le K$.
    \item Every finite-dimensional log-concave law $\mu$ satisfies
          $\CPLit(\mu)\le K\norm{\Cov_\mu}_\op$.
    \item Every finite-dimensional log-concave law $\mu$ satisfies
          $C_{\mathrm P}^{\mathrm{aff}}(\mu)\le K$.
  \end{enumerate}
  If the affine support is $a+V$, then
  $\norm{\Cov_\mu}_\op=\norm{\Cov_{\mu,V}}_\op$ by
  \cref{lem:ambient-intrinsic-covariance}.  The zero-dimensional law is included in all three
  statements.
  In Lean the three statements are \texttt{KLS.IsotropicBound}~$K$,
  \texttt{KLS.CovarianceBound}~$K$ and \texttt{KLS.AffineBound}~$K$, each quantifying over every
  finite-dimensional Euclidean space of one fixed universe; item~(2) is written
  $\CPLit(\mu)\le K\cdot\mathrm{ofReal}\,\norm{\Cov_\mu}_\op$ as in the KLS proposition.  The
  certificate packages the three equivalences and, in addition, that item~(3) for some finite $K$
  on the Euclidean family $\mathbb R^n$ implies the literal proposition \texttt{KLS.Conjecture}.
  The truth of any item for some $K$ is not asserted.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Centering does not change the affine Poincar\'e constant.  In positive dimension, whiten the
  centered intrinsic law by $\Cov_{\mu,V}^{-1/2}$, which exists by
  \cref{lem:ambient-intrinsic-covariance}.  Pullback of the isotropic inequality gives the
  covariance-form inequality and hence item~(3), first on the raw tests and then on their
  relaxation.  Item~(3) implies item~(2) from
  $\inner{\Cov_{\mu,V}v}{v}\le\norm{\Cov_{\mu,V}}_\op\norm{v}^2$, together with cutoff and
  truncation from locally to globally Lipschitz tests.  Item~(2) specializes to item~(1) when
  covariance is the identity.  The zero-dimensional case has zero variance and is immediate; see
  \cite{KannanLovaszSimonovits1995,LeeVempala2018}.
  In Lean, centering is performed on the intrinsic law inside its direction space by the
  translation transport certified with \cref{thm:affine-covariance-relaxation-closed}; the
  whitening is a self-adjoint linear equivalence $W$ satisfying $W\Sigma W=I$, obtained from the
  spectral theorem.  The Lean statement does not assert positivity or uniqueness of $W$; the
  displayed identity suffices for the reduction.  The covariance relaxation and its optimal constant are transported
  along any continuous linear equivalence; the passage between the identity-weighted Lipschitz
  relaxation and the locally Lipschitz literature constant uses the certified smooth approximation
  of \cref{lem:covariance-test-core-density} in place of cutoff and truncation; and the comparison
  $\inner{\Sigma v}{v}\le\norm\Sigma\norm v^2$ is applied to the raw tests and then to the
  relaxations with the factor $\norm\Sigma$.  The zero-dimensional case is included uniformly: the
  positivity premise of the whitening is vacuous and every constant is zero.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[One-dimensional log-concave Poincar\'e bound]
  \label{thm:one-dimensional-logconcave-poincare}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Line.cdf,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Line.map_cdf_eq_uniform,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.exists_lineDensity,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.exists_supergradient,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.mul_le_mul_of_add_eq,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.two_mul_measureReal_Ioi_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.two_mul_cdf_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.le_add_mul_cdf_sub,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.smoothTest_holds,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.one_div_le_variance,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.smoothTest_holds_twelve_variance,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.localSlope_constant_le_twelve_variance,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.localSlope_constant_le_twelve_evariance,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.logConcave_dirac,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Line.affineQuantile,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Line.one_div_le_variance_affineQuantile,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.le_add_mul_cdf_sub_of_mem,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.variance_ge_of_affine_bound,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.cdf_mul_one_sub_cdf_le_sd_mul,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.clip,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.integral_sq_clipCentered_eq,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.groundState_inequality,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.integral_sq_div_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.variance_clip_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.setIntegral_Iic_sq_sub_le_of_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.setIntegral_Ioi_sq_sub_le_of_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LineDensity.variance_le_four_variance_mul,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.smoothTest_holds_four_variance,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.localSlope_constant_le_four_variance,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.localSlope_constant_le_four_evariance,
        Solutions.ThmOneDimensionalLogConcavePoincare.certificate}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:log-concave-law,def:literature-poincare-constant}
  Every log-concave probability law $\nu$ on a line satisfies
  \[
    \CPLit(\nu)\le4\Var_\nu(T),
  \]
  including the zero-variance case.
  In Lean the line is $\mathbb R$, $T$ is the coordinate \texttt{id}, $\CPLit(\nu)$ is
  \texttt{Poincare.LocalSlope.Intrinsic.constant}~$\nu$, and the right-hand side is read in
  the extended reals as \texttt{ENNReal.ofReal}~$(4\Var_\nu(\mathrm{id}))$; the same bound
  through the extended variance is recorded as well.  The zero-variance case is the case of a
  one-point affine support, where the constant is $0$.  Bobkov's constant $12$ is a corollary
  and is also proved independently.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Bobkov's Corollary~4.3, p.~1915 of \cite{Bobkov1999LogConcave}, gives the spectral gap
  $\lambda_1\ge1/(12\Var_\nu(T))$; his equation~(1.3), p.~1904, uses
  $\lambda_1\Var_\nu(f)\le\int|f'|^2\,d\nu$ for locally Lipschitz tests.  The value $4$ is stated
  on p.~11 of \cite{AlonsoGutierrezBastero2015} in every dimension, credited to KLS~1995 and
  without proof there, and is recalled in equation~(2.25) of \cite{CattiauxGuillin2018Poincare}.
  The Lean proof of the
  constant $4$ follows the elementary one-dimensional argument recorded in
  \texttt{research/progress/one-dimensional-poincare.md}; it does not follow
  Bobkov's Cheeger route.  Zero variance means a Dirac law and gives zero variance for every test.

  If the affine support of $\nu$ is a point, the direction space is zero-dimensional and the
  literature constant vanishes.  Otherwise the support has nonempty interior $\Omega$, an open
  interval carrying $\nu$, and the Borell representation gives a density $\rho$, continuous and
  positive on $\Omega$ with concave logarithm.  Let $F$ be the distribution function,
  $W=F(1-F)$, $\sigma^2=\Var_\nu(T)$, and $m$ a median with $c=\rho(m)>0$.

  \emph{Profile bound $W\le\sigma\rho$ on $\Omega$.}  A supporting exponential
  $\rho(s)\le\rho(x_0)e^{k(s-x_0)}$ at a point $x_0\in\Omega$, integrated against the chord
  bounds of $\log\rho$, gives the supporting line $\rho\le A(1-F)+BF$ on $\Omega$ with
  $A=\rho(x_0)-kF(x_0)$, $B=\rho(x_0)+k(1-F(x_0))$, both nonnegative.  For $A,B>0$ let
  $w(u)=A(1-u)+Bu$ and $q(u)=\int_p^u w^{-1}$ on $[0,1]$; $q\circ F$ is $1$-Lipschitz on
  $\Omega$ because $(q\circ F)'=\rho/w(F)\le1$, so, since $F(X)$ is uniform on $[0,1]$ under
  $\nu$ and the mean of $\nu$ lies in $\Omega$, $\Var_{[0,1]}(q)=\Var_\nu(q\circ F)\le\sigma^2$.
  The closed form of $\Var_{[0,1]}(q)$ (through $\int_0^1\log w$ and $\int_0^1\log^2w$) and the
  inequality $ab(\log b-\log a)^2\le(b-a)^2$ give $\Var_{[0,1]}(q)\ge(\sqrt A+\sqrt B)^{-4}$.
  Taking $x_0=x$, perturbing $(A,B)$ to $(A+\varepsilon,B+\varepsilon)$ and letting
  $\varepsilon\to0$ yields $F(x)(1-F(x))\le\sigma\rho(x)$.

  \emph{Sharp Hardy inequality for clipped tests.}  For $a\le b$ in $\Omega$ with
  $F(a)\le\tfrac12\le F(b)$ and a smooth test $f$, let $f_{ab}$ be $f$ clipped to the constants
  $f(a)$ and $f(b)$ outside $[a,b]$, $h=f_{ab}-\int f_{ab}\,d\nu$ and
  $\tilde G(x)=\int_{(-\infty,x]}h\,d\nu$.  Integration by parts gives
  $\int h^2\,d\nu=-\int_a^b f'\tilde G$.  The ground-state identity
  $h^2\rho=\rho\bigl(h-\tilde G(1-2F)/(2W)\bigr)^2+\tilde G^2\rho/(4W^2)+\Xi'$ with
  $\Xi=\tilde G^2(1-2F)/(2W)$, integrated over intervals $[c,e]\subseteq\Omega$ containing
  $[a,b]$ with $F(c)\le\tfrac12\le F(e)$, gives
  $\tfrac14\int_c^e\tilde G^2\rho/W^2\le\int h^2\,d\nu+h(a)^2F(c)+h(b)^2(1-F(e))$ because
  $\tilde G=h(a)F$ left of $a$ and $\tilde G=-h(b)(1-F)$ right of $b$; letting $F(c)\to0$ and
  $F(e)\to1$ gives $\int_a^b\tilde G^2\rho/W^2\le4\int h^2\,d\nu$.  With
  $-f'\tilde G\le\tilde G^2\rho/(8W^2)+2f'^2W^2/\rho$ and the profile bound
  $W^2/\rho\le\sigma^2\rho$ this yields
  $\Var_\nu(f_{ab})=\int h^2\,d\nu\le4\sigma^2\int_a^b f'^2\,d\nu\le4\sigma^2\int f'^2\,d\nu$.

  \emph{Exhaustion.}  The four-point inequality $\rho(a)\rho(e)\le\rho(b)\rho(c)$ for
  $a\le b\le c\le e$, $a+e=b+c$, integrated along translates, gives the monotone hazard rates
  $2c\,(1-F)\le\rho$ on $[m,\infty)$ and $2c\,F\le\rho$ on $(-\infty,m]$.  For an admissible test
  $f$ and $a\le m\le b$ in $\Omega$, integration by parts against $F$ (resp.\ $1-F$), the
  elementary bound $2xy\le c\,x^2+y^2/c$ and a directed union over rational endpoints give the
  anchored half-line bounds $\int_{x\le a}(f-f(a))^2\,d\nu\le c^{-2}\int_{x\le a}f'^2\,d\nu$ and
  $\int_{x>b}(f-f(b))^2\,d\nu\le c^{-2}\int_{x>b}f'^2\,d\nu$, which can be made arbitrarily
  small by absolute continuity of $\int f'^2\,d\nu$, choosing $a,b$ with $F(a)$ and $1-F(b)$
  small.  Since $\int(f-f_{ab})^2\,d\nu$ is bounded by the sum of the two tails and
  $(u+v)^2\le(1+\delta)u^2+(1+\delta^{-1})v^2$ for $\delta>0$,
  $\Var_\nu(f)\le(1+\delta)\Var_\nu(f_{ab})+(1+\delta^{-1})\int(f-f_{ab})^2\,d\nu$; letting the
  tails and then $\delta$ tend to zero yields $\Var_\nu(f)\le4\sigma^2\int f'^2\,d\nu$.  The
  smooth-test optimum equals the literature optimum for log-concave laws
  (\cref{thm:selected-literature-constant-equivalence}).

  \emph{The constant $12$, independently.}  With $a=b=m$ the anchored half-line bounds give
  $\Var_\nu(f)\le\int (f-f(m))^2\,d\nu\le c^{-2}\int (f')^2\,d\nu$.  A supporting exponential
  $\rho(s)\le c\,e^{k(s-m)}$ at the median gives $\rho\le c+k(F-\tfrac12)$ on $\Omega$; hence
  $\varphi(F(x))\le x-m$ for $x\ge m$ and $\varphi(F(x))\ge x-m$ for $x\le m$ with
  $\varphi(u)=(u-\tfrac12)/c-k(u-\tfrac12)^2/(2c^2)$.  Since $F(X)$ is uniform on $[0,1]$
  under $\nu$, $\int (x-m)(F(x)-\tfrac12)\,d\nu\ge\int_0^1\varphi(u)(u-\tfrac12)\,du
  =1/(12c)$, and $ab\le c\,a^2/2+b^2/(2c)$ with $\int (F-\tfrac12)^2\,d\nu=1/12$ yields
  $\Var_\nu\ge1/(12c^2)$, so $c^{-2}\le12\Var_\nu$.
\end{proof}

\noindent\textbf{Remark (sharpness, not Lean-certified here).}
The constant $4$ is optimal: the exponential law has $\CPLit=4\Var$, so no smaller universal
coefficient is possible.  The certificate above proves the upper bound only; the exponential-law
equality and the resulting optimality are not formalized in this repository.  The constant-$12$
route uses two estimates that are sharp for the exponential and uniform laws respectively;
these sharpness assertions are also informal background.

The preceding nodes are deliberately modest.  They do not provide a self-adjoint generator, identify a
form gap with the reciprocal of an operator eigenvalue, or prove any of the four long-route gates.

\begin{theorem}[Bakry--\'Emery Poincar\'e bound]
  \label{thm:bakry-emery-poincare}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.literatureConstant_le_inv_of_eq_tilted,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.smoothTest_holds_inv_of_eq_tilted,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.norm_center_sq_le_inv_mul,
        Solutions.ThmBakryEmeryPoincare.certificate}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:log-concave-law,def:literature-poincare-constant}
  This is Corollary~4.8.2 of \cite{BakryGentilLedoux2014}, in a reviewed stronger form.  Let $V$
  be a finite-dimensional real inner product space with Lebesgue measure, $W:V\to\R$ of class
  $C^2$ with $\nabla^2W(x)(v,v)\ge\rho\norm v^2$ for all $x,v$ and some $\rho>0$, and
  $\nu=e^{-W}dx/\int e^{-W}$ a probability measure.  Then
  \[
    \CPLit(\nu)\le\rho^{-1},
  \]
  and $\Var_\nu(f)\le\rho^{-1}\int\norm{\nabla f}^2\,d\nu$ for every smooth $f\in L^2(\nu)$ with
  $\norm{\nabla f}\in L^2(\nu)$.  The book assumes $V=\R^n$, $W$ smooth and $\int e^{-W}=1$.
  The certificate also constructs the scaled Gaussian $e^{-\rho\norm x^2/2}dx/Z$ as a probability
  measure and applies the bound to it, so the hypotheses are satisfiable in every dimension,
  including dimension zero.  Sharpness of $\rho^{-1}$ and the general Markov-triple
  $CD(\rho,\infty)$ statement (Proposition~4.8.1) are not claimed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The proof follows the book's semigroup route in the Euclidean heat model with generator
  $-\bar T^\dagger\bar T$, where $\bar T$ is the closure of the weighted gradient on $C_c^\infty$.
  The Hessian bound gives $\Gamma_2\ge\rho\Gamma$ and the finite-time decay
  $\Var(u)-\Var(P_tu)\le\rho^{-1}(1-e^{-2\rho t})\norm{\bar Tu}^2$.  Ergodicity follows from
  $\ker\bar T=$ constants (weak gradient and translation invariance) and strong convergence of
  the semigroup on $(\ker\bar T)^\perp$, so $\Var(P_tu)\to0$ and $\Var(u)\le\rho^{-1}\norm{\bar Tu}^2$
  on the form domain.  The optimal constant on the form domain equals the literature constant.
  Log-concavity and full-dimensional support of $\nu$ are derived from the hypotheses.
\end{proof}

\section{First dependencies of the Bizeul--Klartag--Lehec proof}

The following records state the first proved dependencies of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}
(arXiv:2610.05474v1).  Lemma~2.2 is formalized in the source's form; Lemma~2.1, Lemma~3.6 and
Proposition~7.1 have proved restricted counterparts only.  Theorem~3.1 and Theorem~1.1 are not
addressed.

\begin{lemma}[Partial symmetrization of tensors]
  \label{lem:bkl-partial-symmetrization}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.Tensor.norm_le_four_pow_mul_norm_symmetrize,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.Tensor.norm_le_four_pow_mul_norm_blockAverage,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.Tensor.norm_le_choose_mul_norm_blockAverage,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.Tensor.sum_norm_sq_sum_subset_eq}
  \leanok
  This is Lemma~2.2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}.  Let $d\ge0$, $k\ge3d$, and let $A$ be a
  real tensor with $k$ indices in $\{1,\dots,n\}$, with the Hilbert--Schmidt norm.  If $A$ is
  invariant under the permutations of its first $d$ indices and under those of its next $2d$
  indices, then $\abs A\le4^d\abs{S_{2d}A}$, where $S_{2d}$ averages over the permutations of the
  first $2d$ indices and fixes the trailing ones.  The Lean statement holds for every $n$, including
  $n=0$, and the case $d=0$ is included; the representation form holds for every orthogonal
  representation of a finite permutation group with the sharper factor $\binom{2d}{d}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The source's proof: the subset-sum identity~(25) holds for every family of vectors indexed by
  $d$-subsets of a $3d$-set (Vandermonde), its inner sums are $\binom{2d}{d}$ times translates of
  $S_{2d}A$, and $\binom{2d}{d}\le4^d$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Centered inverse of the generator, operator-domain part]
  \label{lem:bkl-centered-inverse}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.Semigroup.Generated.exists_adjointComp_eq_of_gap,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.existsUnique_centered_adjointComp_eq,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.centered_adjointComp_bounds,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.norm_centered_gradient_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.L2.sum_norm_sq_compLp_innerSL,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.existsUnique_centered_adjointComp_eq_components}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:log-concave-law,def:literature-poincare-constant}
  A restricted counterpart of Lemma~2.1 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}.  In the heat model
  $\nu=m.\mathrm{tilted}(-U)$ with $U\in C^2$, $\nu$ log-concave with full-dimensional support, let
  $A=\bar T^\dagger\bar T=-\mathcal L$ be the closed generator.  If $\CPLit(\nu)\le C$, every
  centered $g\in L^2(\nu)$ has exactly one centered preimage $u$ in the operator domain of $A$, and
  $\norm u\le C\norm g$, $\norm{\bar Tu}^2=\langle g,u\rangle\le C\norm g^2$ (BKL~(20)) and
  $\norm{\nabla_0u}\le\sqrt C\norm g$ (BKL~(24)).  Componentwise tensor lift: for a finite-dimensional
  real inner product space $G$ with orthonormal basis $(b_k)$ and centered $g\in L^2(\nu;G)$ there is
  exactly one family of centered $u_k$ in the operator domain with $Au_k=\langle b_k,g\rangle$, and
  $\sum_k\norm{u_k}^2\le C^2\norm g^2$ (Parseval identifies $\sum_k\norm{\langle b_k,g\rangle}^2$ with
  $\norm g^2$).  The source's class $\mathcal A$ of smooth functions of polynomial growth, smooth
  representatives and the pointwise equation are not proved.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The gap inequality on $(\ker\bar T)^\perp$ makes the range of the self-adjoint operator $A$
  closed and dense in $(\ker\bar T)^\perp$; the kernel of $\bar T$ is the constants.  The tensor lift
  applies the scalar result to each component.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Tilt averages of the generator]
  \label{lem:bkl-tilt-generator}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.tiltAverage_neg_weightedLaplacian,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.taylorTensor_neg_weightedLaplacian,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.taylorTensor_one_neg_weightedLaplacian,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.tiltAverage_neg_weightedLaplacian_of_hasTemperateGrowth,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.taylorTensor_neg_weightedLaplacian_of_hasTemperateGrowth,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.taylorTensor_one_neg_weightedLaplacian_of_hasTemperateGrowth,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.taylorTensor_neg_weightedLaplacianVec,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.taylorTensor_one_neg_weightedLaplacianVec,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.iteratedFDeriv_comp_perm_of_contDiff}
  \leanok
  Lemma~3.6 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, in a reviewed stronger form.  Let
  $\mu=m.\mathrm{tilted}(-V)$ with $V\in C^1$ and all exponential moments, $\mu_z$ its
  exponential tilts, $F_f(z)=\int f\,d\mu_z$ and $T_df=\frac1{d!}\nabla^dF_f(0)$.  For a scalar
  $u$ that is either a compactly supported $C^2$ function, or of temperate growth (smooth with every
  derivative polynomially bounded, the source's class $\mathcal A$) when $\nabla V$ is polynomially
  bounded: $F_{-\mathcal Lu}(z)=\langle z,F_{\nabla u}(z)\rangle$ for every $z$ (BKL~(48)),
  $T_1(-\mathcal Lu)=\int\nabla u\,d\mu$ (49) and $T_{d+1}(-\mathcal Lu)=S_{d+1}T_d(\nabla u)$ for
  every $d$ (50), the original index of $T_d(\nabla u)$ placed after its Taylor indices.  The same
  holds for tensor-valued $u\in\mathcal A$ with values in a finite-dimensional real normed space $F$,
  with $\mathcal Lu=\Delta u-Du(\nabla V)$ componentwise: $T_{d+1}(-\mathcal Lu)$ is $S_{d+1}$ of the
  $F$-valued $(d+1)$-linear map $T_d(Du)$ with the derivative index after the Taylor indices, the
  indices carried by $F$ trailing and not symmetrized.  The source's regular measures satisfy these
  hypotheses (an informal check, not formalized); the Lean statement drops convexity and the Hessian
  bounds and includes $d=0$.  The tensors are continuous multilinear maps; their operator norm is
  not the source's Hilbert--Schmidt norm (see \cref{lem:bkl-taylor-coefficients}).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Integration by parts under each tilt gives~(48); for tests of temperate growth, a scaled bump
  cutoff and dominated convergence remove the support condition.  Differentiating under the integral sign shows
  that $F_{\nabla u}$ is smooth; the Leibniz rule with a linear factor and the symmetry of
  iterated derivatives of $C^\infty$ maps (proved here, since Mathlib states it only for analytic
  maps) give~(49) and~(50); the tensor-valued case follows from the scalar one through continuous
  linear functionals on $F$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Taylor tensors of linear functions are cumulants]
  \label{lem:bkl-linear-cumulant}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.taylorTensor_inner_eq_cumulant,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.tiltAverage_inner_eq_fderiv_logLaplace}
  \leanok
  A step of the proof of Proposition~7.1 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}.  For a nonzero finite
  measure $\mu$ with all exponential moments and $\Lambda_\mu(z)=\log\int e^{\langle z,x\rangle}d\mu$,
  $F_{\langle v,\cdot\rangle}=D\Lambda_\mu(\cdot)v$ and
  $T_d\langle v,\cdot\rangle=\kappa_{d+1}(v,\cdot,\dots,\cdot)/d!$ with
  $\kappa_m=\nabla^m\Lambda_\mu(0)$.  Proposition~7.1 itself is not addressed.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Differentiation under the integral sign of the Laplace transform and of its logarithm.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Coefficient arrays of Taylor tensors]
  \label{lem:bkl-taylor-coefficients}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.Tensor.coordTensor_symmetrizeML,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.Tensor.coordTensor_appendInner,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.Tensor.norm_le_norm_coordTensor,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.slotPerm_coordTensor_taylorTensor}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-partial-symmetrization,lem:bkl-tilt-generator}
  The tensor conventions of Section~2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}.  In an orthonormal basis
  $(b_i)$ of $E$, a real $k$-linear map $\Phi$ has the coefficient array
  $(\Phi(b_{i_1},\dots,b_{i_k}))_i$, a tensor with the Hilbert--Schmidt norm.  The array of the full
  symmetrization of $\Phi$ is $S_k$ of the array of $\Phi$; pairing a vector-valued $d$-linear map with
  a last argument places the original index after the Taylor indices; the operator norm of $\Phi$ is
  at most the Hilbert--Schmidt norm of its array; and the arrays of Taylor tensors $T_df$ are
  symmetric whenever $F_f$ is smooth, so Lemma~2.2 applies to them.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Multilinear expansion in the basis, Cauchy--Schwarz and Parseval; symmetry of iterated
  derivatives of $C^\infty$ maps.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Taylor tensors on $L^2$]
  \label{lem:bkl-taylor-l2}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.contDiff_tiltAverage_of_memLp,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.iteratedFDeriv_integral_exp_inner_smul,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.taylorTensor_add,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.exists_norm_taylorTensor_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-tilt-generator}
  For a probability measure $\mu$ with all exponential moments and $f\in L^2(\mu)$, the tilt average
  $F_f$ is smooth, $\nabla^d$ of $z\mapsto\int e^{\langle z,x\rangle}g\,d\mu$ is the weighted moment
  $\int e^{\langle z,x\rangle}\prod_j\langle x,h_j\rangle g\,d\mu$, $f\mapsto T_df$ is linear, and
  $\norm{T_df}\le C_d\norm f_{L^2(\mu)}$ (operator norm).  This is the continuity of $T_d$ on $L^2$
  used in Proposition~7.1 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026} and needed to state hypothesis~(29) of its
  Theorem~3.1.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Differentiation under the integral sign with exponential domination; the Leibniz bound for
  $F_f=(1/D)\,N_f$ at $0$, the moment formula and Cauchy--Schwarz.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Integration by parts on the class $\mathcal A$]
  \label{lem:bkl-ibp-temperate}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_weightedLaplacian_mul_eq_neg_of_hasCompactSupport,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_weightedLaplacian_mul_eq_neg_of_hasTemperateGrowth,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_weightedLaplacian_mul_comm_of_hasTemperateGrowth,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_inner_weightedLaplacianVec_eq_neg}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-tilt-generator}
  Equation~(15) of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026} for scalar functions.  Let $\mu=m.\mathrm{tilted}(-V)$
  with $V\in C^1$, $\nabla V$ of polynomial growth and all exponential moments.  For $u\in C^2$ and
  a compactly supported $w\in C^2$, $\int(\mathcal Lu)w\,d\mu=-\int\langle\nabla u,\nabla w\rangle\,d\mu$;
  for $u,v$ of temperate growth (the class $\mathcal A$),
  $\int(\mathcal Lu)v\,d\mu=-\int\langle\nabla u,\nabla v\rangle\,d\mu=\int u(\mathcal Lv)\,d\mu$.  For
  tensor fields of temperate growth with values in a finite-dimensional real inner product space,
  $\int\langle\mathcal Lu,v\rangle\,d\mu=-\int\sum_i\langle Du(e_i),Dv(e_i)\rangle\,d\mu$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Locality of $\mathcal L$ reduces the compact case to the existing integration by parts for a
  compactly supported first factor; a scaled bump cutoff and dominated convergence give the
  temperate case.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Integrated Bochner formula on the class $\mathcal A$]
  \label{lem:bkl-bochner-temperate}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_gammaTwo_eq_of_hasTemperateGrowth,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_weightedLaplacian_sq_eq_of_hasTemperateGrowth,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_hessianSq_add_le_of_hasTemperateGrowth,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_norm_sq_weightedLaplacianVec_eq,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_hessian_add_le_norm_sq_weightedLaplacianVec}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-ibp-temperate}
  Equation~(16) of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026} for scalar functions.  Let $\mu=m.\mathrm{tilted}(-V)$
  with $V$ of temperate growth and all exponential moments.  For $u$ of temperate growth,
  $\int\Gamma_2(u)\,d\mu=\int(\mathcal Lu)^2d\mu$ and
  $\int(\mathcal Lu)^2d\mu=\int\norm{\nabla^2u}_{HS}^2d\mu+\int\nabla^2V(\nabla u,\nabla u)\,d\mu$; if
  $\nabla^2V\ge\rho$, then $\int\norm{\nabla^2u}_{HS}^2d\mu+\rho\int\norm{\nabla u}^2d\mu\le\int(\mathcal Lu)^2d\mu$,
  the inequality behind Lemma~3.4.  For tensor fields of temperate growth (componentwise $\mathcal L$),
  $\int\norm{\mathcal Lu}^2d\mu=\int\sum_{i,j}\norm{\nabla^2u(e_i,e_j)}^2d\mu
  +\int\sum_{i,j}\nabla^2V(e_i,e_j)\langle Du(e_i),Du(e_j)\rangle\,d\mu$, with the same curvature form.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The class of functions of temperate growth is closed under $\nabla$, $\Delta$, $\mathcal L$ and
  $\Gamma$; $\int\mathcal L\Gamma(u,u)\,d\mu=0$ and~(15) for $(u,\mathcal Lu)$ give the first identity,
  and the pointwise Bochner formula gives the second.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Taylor tensors of the iterates]
  \label{lem:bkl-corollary-3-7}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.symmetrizeML_taylorTensor_centeredFDeriv}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-tilt-generator}
  Corollary~3.7 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, in a reviewed stronger form.  Let $h$ be a tensor
  field of temperate growth and $g$, $\alpha$ such that $-\mathcal Lh=\alpha\nabla_0g$, where
  $\nabla_0u=Du-\int Du\,d\mu$ (the relation~(30) defining $h_{i+1}$ from $h_i$).  Then for $d\ge1$,
  $S_{d+1}T_d(\nabla_0h)=T_{d+1}(-\mathcal Lh)=\alpha\,T_{d+1}(\nabla_0g)$.  The existence and the
  normalization~(31) of the source's iterates, which use Lemma~2.1 on $\mathcal A$, are not proved.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Lemma~3.6 for tensor fields, $T_d$ of a constant vanishes for $d\ge1$, and homogeneity of $T_{d+1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Convex potential of the suspension]
  \label{lem:bkl-suspension-convexity}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.potential,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.convexOn_potential,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.exists_copies,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.continuous_potential}
  \leanok
  A step of Proposition~7.1 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, equations~(109)--(110).
