  Let $V,f\in C^2(E;\mathbb R)$, $\nabla^2V\ge a\,\mathrm{Id}$,
  $\norm{D^2f(x)}_{\mathrm{op}}\le M$, and $\beta\ge0$.
  For every positive $N\in\mathbb N$ satisfying $\beta M/\sqrt N\le a$ and every
  $\sigma\in\mathbb R$, the continuous potential
  \[
    W_N(s,x_1,\ldots,x_N)=\sum_i V(x_i)+
       \beta\left|\sigma s-\frac{1}{\sqrt N}\sum_i f(x_i)\right|
  \]
  is convex. If $a>0$ and $M\ge0$, a positive finite $N$ satisfying this
  condition exists. The paper uses $\sigma=\sqrt{1+2/\beta^2}$ and $\beta>0$.
  This proves neither the identification with the joint law nor its isotropy,
  the mixed cumulant identity, or Proposition~7.1.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The Hessian bound implies convexity of $V\pm\beta f/\sqrt N$.
  Express $W_N$ as the maximum of their finite sums plus the corresponding
  linear term in $s$. Choose $N>(\beta M/a)^2+1$ for the existence claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The Laplace law]
  \label{lem:bkl-laplace-law}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Line.laplaceDensity,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Line.laplaceMeasure,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Line.isProbabilityMeasure_laplaceMeasure,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Line.integral_exp_mul_laplaceDensity,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Line.laplaceMeasure_mgf,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Line.laplaceMeasure_moments,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Line.integrable_pow_laplaceMeasure,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Line.integrable_exp_abs_laplaceMeasure,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Line.hasDerivAt_log_integral_exp_laplaceMeasure_zero}
  \leanok
  The noise of Proposition~7.1 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~28. For $\beta>0$ the
  Laplace law $\eta_\beta$ with density $(\beta/2)e^{-\beta|s|}$ is a probability measure with
  $\mathbb E\,e^{w\eta_\beta}=\beta^2/(\beta^2-w^2)$ for $|w|<\beta$ (and no exponential moment
  of order $\ge\beta$), mean $0$, second moment $2/\beta^2$, all polynomial moments, and
  $\frac{d}{dw}\log\mathbb E\,e^{(w/\sigma)\eta_\beta}\big|_{w=0}=0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Direct integration of the two exponential halves; the moments come from the analytic
  moment generating function on $(-\beta,\beta)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The joint law of the suspension]
  \label{lem:bkl-suspension-law}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.JointSpace,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.sigma,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.copyStat,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.embed,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.jointLaw,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.isProbabilityMeasure_jointLaw_of_pos,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.jointLaw_eq_tilted,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.logConcave_jointLaw,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.exists_integrable_exp_norm_contDiffOn_logLaplace_jointLaw}
  \leanok
  Proposition~7.1 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, equations~(109)--(110), p.~28--29. Let
  $X_1,\dots,X_N$ be independent with law $\mu=e^{-V}dx/Z$ and $\eta_\beta$ an independent
  Laplace variable; with $F_N=N^{-1/2}\sum f(X_i)$ and $\sigma_\beta^2=1+2/\beta^2$, the joint law
  $\nu$ of $(S,X_1,\dots,X_N)$, $S=(F_N+\eta_\beta)/\sigma_\beta$, on
  $\mathbb R^{\{0\}\sqcup([N]\times[n])}$ is a probability measure with density proportional to
  $e^{-W_N}$ for every measurable $V,f$ and every $N,n$ (109). If $V,f\in C^2$,
  $\nabla^2V\ge a$, $\norm{\nabla^2f}\le M$ and $\beta M/\sqrt N\le a$ (110), then $\nu$ is
  log-concave, has an exponential moment, and $\Lambda_\nu$ is $C^\infty$ near $0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Product densities, a measurable fibrewise shear with density multiplier $\sigma$, a
  volume-preserving coordinate equivalence and normalization give~(109); convexity of $W_N$
  (Lemma~\ref{lem:bkl-suspension-convexity}) gives log-concavity; Borell's tail bound gives the
  exponential moment, and the local smoothness of logarithmic Laplace transforms the rest.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Isotropy of the joint law]
  \label{lem:bkl-suspension-isotropy}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Probability.pi_normalizedSum_moments,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.integral_mul_comp_eval_pi,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.prod_add_moments,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.copyStat_moments,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.scalar_moments,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.integral_scalar_mul_inner,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.jointLaw_moments_of_laplace,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.isotropic_jointLaw}
  \leanok
  Proposition~7.1 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, the isotropy computation from~(108),
  p.~28: if $\mu$ is centered with identity covariance and $f\in L^2(\mu)$ satisfies
  $\int f=0$, $\int f^2=1$ and $\int\langle u,x\rangle f=0$ for all $u$, then for every
  $N\ge1$ and $\beta>0$ the joint law is isotropic.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Second moments of normalized sums of independent copies, of independent sums, and of the
  coordinate products, assembled coordinatewise and transported through the embedding.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Derivatives of the joint logarithmic Laplace transform]
  \label{lem:bkl-suspension-derivatives}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.lintegral_exp_inner_jointLaw,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.logLaplace_jointLaw_factorization,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.exists_graph_norm_moment_from_joint,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.fderiv_logLaplace_jointLaw_none,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.cumulant_jointLaw_none_entries,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.contDiffOn_logLaplace_ball,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.tiltAverage_inner_eq_fderiv_logLaplace_of_lt,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.iteratedFDeriv_comp_clm_of_isOpen,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.iteratedFDeriv_comp_perm_of_contDiffOn}
  \leanok
  Proposition~7.1 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, equations~(111)--(112), p.~29, for a regular
  law $\mu$, $f\in C^2$ with $\norm{\nabla^2f}\le M$ and $N$ chosen by~(110):
  $\Lambda_\nu$ factorizes into the noise term and the copy terms; at points with vanishing
  $S$-coordinate, $\partial_{e_0}\Lambda_\nu(0,z_1,\dots,z_N)=(\sigma\sqrt N)^{-1}\sum_iF_f(z_i)$;
  and for $d\ge1$ the mixed cumulants $\kappa^\nu_{d+1}(e_0,e_{(i_1,j_1)},\dots,e_{(i_d,j_d)})$
  vanish unless all $i_k$ coincide, in which case they equal
  $\frac{d!}{\sigma\sqrt N}\,T_df(e_{j_1},\dots,e_{j_d})$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Local smoothness of $\Lambda$ on the ball given by one exponential moment, the chain rule along
  continuous linear block maps, symmetry of the iterated derivatives of a locally smooth map, and the
  differentiation of the copy terms through the graph law $(f(x),x)_*\mu$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Coordinate adapter and the Hilbert--Schmidt step]
  \label{lem:bkl-coordinate-adapter}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isotropic.map_linearIsometryEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.cumulant_map_linearIsometryEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.coordinateEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.cumulantBound_jointSpace,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.Tensor.sum_sq_blocks_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.sum_sq_cumulant_ge_of_entries,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.le_of_forall_le_mul_sigma_sq}
  \leanok
  Proposition~7.1 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, pp.~29--30. Isotropy and cumulants transport
  along linear isometries, so the hypothesis $R^\nu_{d+1}\le b\,\mathrm{Id}$ ``in every
  dimension'' applies to the joint law indexed by $\{0\}\sqcup([N]\times[n])$; summing the
  squares of the entries of~(112) gives $(d!)^2\sigma^{-2}|T_df|^2\le\sum_K\kappa^\nu_{d+1}(e_0,e_K)^2$;
  and a bound $X\le C\sigma_\beta^2$ for all $\beta>0$ gives $X\le C$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Pointwise transport $\Lambda_{\varphi_*\nu}=\Lambda_\nu\circ\varphi^{-1}$, reindexing of finite
  sums, and $\sigma_\beta^2=1+2/\beta^2\to1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Affine-orthogonal decomposition in $L^2(\mu)$]
  \label{lem:bkl-affine-orthogonal}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.L2.affineOrthPart,
        FunctionalInequalities.L2.integral_affineOrthPart,
        FunctionalInequalities.L2.integral_inner_mul_affineOrthPart,
        FunctionalInequalities.L2.memLp_affineOrthPart,
        FunctionalInequalities.L2.contDiff_affineOrthPart,
        FunctionalInequalities.L2.eLpNorm_sub_affineOrthPart_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.L2.integral_affineOrthPart_sq_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.L2.norm_apply_le_sqrt_two_mul,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.norm_coordTensor_taylorTensor_inner_sq_le_of_basis}
  \leanok
  Proposition~7.1 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, end of the proof, p.~30. For a centered
  probability law with identity covariance, $\tilde g=g-\int g-\sum_j(\int\langle e_j,x\rangle g)\langle e_j,\cdot\rangle$
  is centered, orthogonal to the linear functions, in $L^2$, $C^2$ with the Hessian of $g$ when
  $g\in C^2$, a contraction towards affine-orthogonal $f$, and $\int\tilde g^2\le\int g^2$; the
  linear-function step $|T_d\langle v,\cdot\rangle|^2\le b|v|^2/(d!)^2$ holds in any orthonormal basis.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Orthonormality of $\{1,\langle e_j,\cdot\rangle\}$ in $L^2(\mu)$ and $T_d\langle v,\cdot\rangle=\kappa_{d+1}(v,\cdot)/d!$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The Taylor-coefficient bound on the affine-orthogonal class]
  \label{lem:bkl-suspension-taylor-bound}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.CumulantBound,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.norm_coordTensor_taylorTensor_sq_le_of_cumulantBound,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.norm_coordTensor_taylorTensor_sq_le_of_smooth,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.norm_coordTensor_taylorTensor_sq_le_of_orthogonal}
  \leanok
  Proposition~7.1 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, equation~(113), pp.~29--30. Assume $d\ge2$, $b>0$ and $R^\nu_{d+1}\le b\,\mathrm{Id}$ for every isotropic
  log-concave probability measure $\nu$ in every dimension. Then for every isotropic regular law
  $\mu$ and every $f\in C^2(\mathbb R^n)\cap L^2(\mu)$ with bounded Hessian satisfying~(108),
  $|T_df|^2\le b/(d!)^2$ in the Hilbert--Schmidt norm; by density (smooth compactly supported
  approximants and their affine-orthogonal parts) the homogeneous form
  $|T_df|^2\le b\,\norm f_{L^2}^2/(d!)^2$ holds for every $f\in L^2(\mu)$ orthogonal to the affine
  functions. The bound~(107) with the factor~$2$ is not part of this statement.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  For each $\beta>0$ choose $N$ with $\beta M/\sqrt N\le a$; the joint law of the suspension is
  isotropic (Lemma~\ref{lem:bkl-suspension-isotropy}) and log-concave
  (Lemma~\ref{lem:bkl-suspension-law}), so the hypothesis applies on $\mathbb R^{Nn+1}$
  (Lemma~\ref{lem:bkl-coordinate-adapter}); the entries~(112)
  (Lemma~\ref{lem:bkl-suspension-derivatives}) and the Hilbert--Schmidt step give
  $(d!)^2\sigma_\beta^{-2}|T_df|^2\le b$, and $\beta\to\infty$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[BKL Proposition 7.1]
  \label{prop:bkl-suspension}
  \lean{Solutions.PropBklSuspension.certificate,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.CumulantBound,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.Suspension.norm_coordTensor_taylorTensor_sq_le_two_mul}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-suspension-law, lem:bkl-suspension-isotropy, lem:bkl-suspension-derivatives,
        lem:bkl-coordinate-adapter, lem:bkl-affine-orthogonal, lem:bkl-suspension-taylor-bound}
  Proposition~7.1 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, equation~(107), in the source form. Let $d\ge2$
  and $b>0$, and assume $R^\nu_{d+1}\le b\,\mathrm{Id}$ for every isotropic log-concave probability
  measure $\nu$ in every dimension (\texttt{CumulantBound d b}). Then every isotropic regular law
  $\mu$ on $\mathbb R^n$ satisfies $|T_df|^2\le 2b\,\norm f_{L^2(\mu)}^2/(d!)^2$ for every
  $f\in L^2(\mu)$, in the Hilbert--Schmidt norm of the coefficient array. The hypothesis is the
  source's own universal cumulant hypothesis; it is not asserted here. Theorem~4.1, which
  supplies it, is Theorem~\ref{thm:bkl-cumulant-bound}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Decompose $f=c+\ell+h$ (constant, linear, affine-orthogonal). $T_dc=0$ for $d\ge1$; the
  linear-function step bounds $|T_d\ell|^2\le b|v|^2/(d!)^2$ from the hypothesis in dimension $n$;
  Lemma~\ref{lem:bkl-suspension-taylor-bound} bounds $|T_dh|^2\le b\norm h^2/(d!)^2$; and
  Pythagoras gives $|v|^2+\norm h^2\le\norm f^2$, so $|T_df|^2\le2(|T_d\ell|^2+|T_dh|^2)$ yields~(107).
\end{proof}

\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}[Small quadratic tilts]
  \label{lem:bkl-quadratic-tilt}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Probability.integral_quadraticTilt_tendsto,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.quadraticTiltLaw_tendsto,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.integral_norm_sq_quadraticTilt_tendsto}
  \leanok
  Section~2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~5 (second step of the regular approximation):
  for a probability measure $\mu$ on a normed space, the normalized tilts
  $e^{-\delta\|x\|^2/2}\mu/Z_\delta$ converge to $\mu$ as $\delta\to0^+$ against integrable
  observables, weakly, and in first and second moments. The Gaussian smoothing step and the
  joint limit are not covered.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Dominated convergence.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Weak-limit transfer of the Poincaré bound]
  \label{lem:bkl-weak-limit-transfer}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.SmoothTest.variance_le_mul_energy_of_tendsto,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.SmoothTest.Admissible.of_contDiff_hasCompactSupport,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.SmoothTest.tendsto_integral_scaled_mul,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.SmoothTest.norm_gradient_scaled_mul_sq_eq,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.SmoothTest.norm_gradient_scaled_mul_sq_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.SmoothTest.tendsto_energy_scaled_mul,
        FunctionalInequalities.Poincare.SmoothTest.Holds.of_compactSupport,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.localSlope_constant_le_of_tendsto}
  \leanok
  Section~2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~5 (the reduction of Theorem~1.1 to regular laws).
  Let $\mu_j\to\mu$ weakly on a finite-dimensional real inner product space, let each $\mu_j$
  satisfy $\operatorname{Var}_{\mu_j}f\le C_j\int\|\nabla f\|^2\,d\mu_j$ on smooth tests with
  finite energy, and let $C_j\to C$. Then the same bound holds for $\mu$: first on compactly
  supported smooth $f$ by weak convergence of the bounded continuous integrands, then on every
  admissible $f$ by the cutoffs $f\chi_{n}$ with $\chi_n=\chi(\cdot/n)$ (whose $L^2$ data and
  energies converge). If $\mu$ is log-concave, its literature Poincaré constant is at most $C$.
  The approximating laws themselves are not constructed here.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Weak convergence gives $\int f\,d\mu_j\to\int f\,d\mu$, $\int f^2\,d\mu_j\to\int f^2\,d\mu$ and
  $\int\|\nabla f\|^2\,d\mu_j\to\int\|\nabla f\|^2\,d\mu$ for compactly supported smooth $f$, so the
  inequality passes to the limit with $C_j\to C$. For admissible $f$, $f\chi_n$ is smooth with
  compact support, $\nabla(f\chi_n)=\nabla f$ on $\|x\|<n$ and
  $\|\nabla(f\chi_n)\|^2\le2\|\nabla f\|^2+2(M/n)^2f^2$, so dominated convergence gives the
  variance and energy limits. The smooth-test constant equals the literature constant for
  log-concave laws.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Upper Hessian bound of the Gaussian-smoothed potential]
  \label{lem:bkl-gaussian-hessian-upper}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.normalizedDensity_line_factorization,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.potential_line_cgf,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.bilinearIteratedFDerivTwo_potential_eq_sub_variance_score,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.bilinearIteratedFDerivTwo_potential_eq_sub_variance_inner,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.bilinearIteratedFDerivTwo_potential_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.variance_inner_posterior_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.memLp_two_id_posterior}
  \leanok
  Section~2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~5 (regular approximation: $\nabla^2V_\varepsilon\le\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{Id}$
  for the Gaussian convolution of variance~$\varepsilon$). Let $\mu$ be a probability law with a
  Borell density $\rho$ on a finite-dimensional real inner product space and $s>0$; the smoothing
  kernel $e^{-s\|x-y\|^2}$ has variance $\varepsilon=1/(2s)$. With the Gaussian posterior
  $P_x=\mu\text{ tilted by }e^{-s\|x-\cdot\|^2}$ and the score $X=2s\langle\cdot-x,v\rangle$, the
  potential $V_s=-\log(\rho*\gamma_s)$ satisfies, for all $x,v$,
  $\nabla^2V_s(x)(v,v)=2s\|v\|^2-\operatorname{Var}_{P_x}X=2s\|v\|^2-4s^2\operatorname{Var}_{P_x}\langle\cdot,v\rangle\le2s\|v\|^2$,
  and, by convexity of $V_s$, $\operatorname{Var}_{P_x}\langle\cdot,v\rangle\le\|v\|^2/(2s)$ with $P_x$
  of finite second moment. No moment hypothesis on $\mu$ is used. Polynomial growth of all
  derivatives and the covariance normalization are not treated here.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Completing the square, $e^{-s\|x-y\|^2}e^{tX(y)}=e^{st^2\|v\|^2}e^{-s\|x+tv-y\|^2}$, so every
  exponential moment of $X$ under $P_x$ is finite and $(\rho*\gamma_s)(x+tv)=(\rho*\gamma_s)(x)\,e^{-st^2\|v\|^2}\,\mathrm{mgf}_{P_x}X(t)$.
  Taking logarithms, $V_s(x+tv)=V_s(x)+st^2\|v\|^2-\mathrm{cgf}_{P_x}X(t)$; the second derivative at
  $t=0$ is $2s\|v\|^2-\operatorname{Var}_{P_x}X$, and equals the Hessian in direction $v$ since
  $V_s\in C^2$. Nonnegativity of the variance gives the upper bound; nonnegativity of the Hessian
  (convexity) gives the posterior variance bound. The second moments follow from the exponential
  domain and an orthonormal-basis sum.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Quantitative Borell moment bounds]
  \label{lem:bkl-borell-uniform-moments}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.BorelLogConcave.integral_exp_norm_le_of_ball,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.BorelLogConcave.integral_norm_pow_le_of_ball,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.measure_ball_ge_of_integral_norm_sq_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.BorelLogConcave.integral_norm_pow_le_of_second_moment,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.BorelLogConcave.integral_norm_pow_le_of_second_bound,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isotropic.integral_norm_sq,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.integral_norm_pow_le_of_isotropic}
  \leanok
  Supporting the regular approximation of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, Section~2, p.~5 (uniform
  moments). Let $\nu$ be a Borel log-concave probability law on a normed space and suppose the ball
  of radius $R>0$ carries mass at least $3/4$. With $q=\log 3/(4R)$,
  $\int e^{q\|v\|}\,d\nu\le C_0:=3^{3/4}/(1-3^{-1/4})$ and $\int\|v\|^p\,d\nu\le p!\,q^{-p}C_0$ for
  every $p$. If $\int\|v\|^2\,d\nu\le R^2/4$ the ball has mass at least $3/4$ (Markov); if
  $\int\|v\|^2\,d\nu\le M$ one may take $R=2\sqrt{M+1}$. Isotropic log-concave laws on
  $\mathbb R^n$ have $\int\|v\|^2=n$, hence all moments bounded by constants depending on $n$ and
  $p$ only. The constants are explicit and uniform over all such laws.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Borell's tail lemma gives $\nu(\|v\|\ge kR)\le 3^{(1-k)/2}$; dominate $e^{q\|v\|}$ by the shell
  constants $e^{q(k+1)R}$ and sum the geometric series with ratio $3^{-1/4}$. The moment bound
  is $\|v\|^p\le p!\,q^{-p}e^{q\|v\|}$. Markov's inequality on $\|v\|^2$ gives the ball mass, and
  the radius $2\sqrt{M+1}$ satisfies $M\le R^2/4$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Gaussian posterior of the smoothing and its uniform moments]
  \label{lem:bkl-gaussian-posterior-moments}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.posteriorLaw,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.posteriorLaw_isBorellDensity,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.posteriorLaw_logConcave,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.centeredPosteriorLaw,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.integral_id_centeredPosteriorLaw,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.variance_inner_centeredPosteriorLaw_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.integral_norm_sq_centeredPosteriorLaw_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.integral_norm_pow_centeredPosteriorLaw_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.integral_exp_norm_centeredPosteriorLaw_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-gaussian-hessian-upper, lem:bkl-borell-uniform-moments}
  Section~2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~5 (moment input for the polynomial growth of the
  derivatives of the smoothed potential). For a probability law $\mu$ with a Borell density
  $\rho$ on a finite-dimensional inner product space, $s>0$ and $x$, the posterior
  $P_x=\mu$ tilted by $e^{-s\|x-\cdot\|^2}$ is a log-concave probability law with density
  $\rho(y)e^{-s\|x-y\|^2}/Z_x$. Its centering $Q_x$ has mean zero,
  $\operatorname{Var}_{Q_x}\langle\cdot,v\rangle\le\|v\|^2/(2s)$ and $\int\|y\|^2\,dQ_x\le n/(2s)$;
  hence, with $R=2\sqrt{n/(2s)+1}$ and $q=\log3/(4R)$, $\int e^{q\|y\|}\,dQ_x\le C_0$ and
  $\int\|y\|^p\,dQ_x\le p!\,q^{-p}C_0$ for all $p$, uniformly in $x$. The growth of the
  derivatives themselves is not treated here.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The density is the product of $\rho$ with a positive continuous log-concave weight, so it is
  upper semicontinuous and log-concave, and the measure identity is `withDensity` multiplicativity.
  The posterior has finite second moment (Lemma~\ref{lem:bkl-gaussian-hessian-upper}), so its
  barycenter is integrable and the translated law is centered; variances are translation
  invariant and bounded by the Hessian identity; summing over an orthonormal basis gives the
  trace. Lemma~\ref{lem:bkl-borell-uniform-moments} with $M=n/(2s)$ gives the uniform moments.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Polynomial growth of the derivatives of the smoothed potential]
  \label{lem:bkl-gaussian-growth}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.iteratedFDeriv_weightedExp_integral_of_lt,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.norm_iteratedFDeriv_laplace_zero_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.norm_iteratedFDeriv_log_comp_le_factorial_sq,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.hasTemperateGrowth_of_bounded_high_derivatives,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.potential_centered_factorization,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.potential_iteratedFDeriv_ge_three,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.exists_uniform_potential_derivative_bound,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.potential_hasTemperateGrowth}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-gaussian-posterior-moments}
  Section~2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~5: every derivative of $V_s=-\log(\rho*\gamma_s)$
  has polynomial growth. Along an increment $u$ at $x$, with the centered posterior $Q_x$ and its
  mean displacement $m_x-x$, $V_s(x+u)=V_s(x)+s\|u\|^2-2s\langle m_x-x,u\rangle-\Lambda_{Q_x}(2su)$,
  hence $D^nV_s(x)=-(2s)^nD^n\Lambda_{Q_x}(0)$ for $n\ge3$. The Laplace derivatives of $Q_x$ at $0$
  are its moment tensors, bounded by the uniform Borell moments; the local log-composition bound
  gives $\|D^n\Lambda_{Q_x}(0)\|\le(n!)^2D^n$ with $D$ independent of $x$; and uniformly bounded
  derivatives of all orders $\ge3$ give \texttt{HasTemperateGrowth}. Constants depend on $n$, the
  dimension and $s$ only.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Completing the square gives all exponential moments of $Q_x$ and the factorization; the
  quadratic polynomial has vanishing derivatives of order $\ge3$; differentiate under the integral
  inside one exponential radius; apply Faà di Bruno to $\log$ at the normalized value $1$; descend
  from order $3$ to orders $2,1,0$ by the mean value inequality.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The regular approximation produces regular potentials]
  \label{lem:bkl-regular-producer}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.bilinearIteratedFDerivTwo_add_quadratic,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.of_convexOn_hasTemperateGrowth,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.quadraticTilt_smoothedLaw_eq_tilted,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.regularPotential_quadraticPotential,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.regularLaw_eq_quadraticTilt,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.quadraticTilt_logConcave}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-gaussian-hessian-upper, lem:bkl-gaussian-growth, lem:bkl-quadratic-tilt}
  Section~2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~5, and the regular class~(12). For a probability
  law with a Borell density, $s>0$ and $\delta>0$, the potential $V_s+\tfrac\delta2\|\cdot\|^2$ is a
  regular potential with $\delta\,\mathrm{Id}\le\nabla^2\le(2s+\delta)\,\mathrm{Id}$ (the Gaussian
  variance is $\varepsilon=1/(2s)$), its law is exactly the two-step law (Gaussian smoothing, then
  the quadratic tilt), and that law is log-concave. The approximating sequence and its convergence
  are not treated here.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Adding $\tfrac\delta2\|\cdot\|^2$ adds $\delta\|v\|^2$ to the Hessian quadratic form; the lower
  bound is convexity of $V_s$, the upper bound is Lemma~\ref{lem:bkl-gaussian-hessian-upper},
  temperate growth is Lemma~\ref{lem:bkl-gaussian-growth}; the law identity is the tilt of a tilt.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Regular potentials under affine changes of variables]
  \label{lem:bkl-regular-affine}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.bilinearIteratedFDerivTwo_comp_affine,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.map_tilted_measurableEquiv,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.comp_affine,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.inverseAffine_map_volume,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.law_comp_affine,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.integral_exp_neg_comp_affine,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.law_comp_affine_eq_map}
  \leanok
  Section~2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~5 (the regular class~(12) under centering and
  whitening). For a regular potential $V$ with $a\,\mathrm{Id}\le\nabla^2V\le b\,\mathrm{Id}$ and an
  invertible affine map $x\mapsto Lx+c$, the potential $V(L\cdot+c)$ is regular with
  $\nabla^2\ge a/(1+\|L^{-1}\|)^2$ and $\nabla^2\le\max(b,1)(1+\|L\|)^2$ (positive constants in
  every dimension, including zero), its normalizer is $|\det L|^{-1}$ times that of $V$, and its
  law is the image of the law of $V$ under $y\mapsto L^{-1}(y-c)$, i.e.\ a translation followed by
  the continuous linear equivalence $L^{-1}$. No whitening operator is chosen here.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Hessian chain rule for affine maps and operator-norm bounds; the tilted law is transported
  through the measurable equivalence $y\mapsto L^{-1}(y-c)$, under which Lebesgue measure scales
  by a positive constant that cancels in the normalization.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Moments and weak convergence of the Gaussian smoothing]
  \label{lem:bkl-gaussian-moments}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Gaussian.laplace,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Gaussian.mean_zero,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Gaussian.integral_inner_sq,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.smoothedLaw_mean,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.smoothedLaw_covariance_operator,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.smoothedLaw_norm_sq,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.smoothedLaw_covariance_operator_tendsto,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.smoothedProbabilityLaw_tendsto}
  \leanok
  Section~2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~5. The Gaussian noise $\gamma_s$ with kernel
  $e^{-s\|y\|^2}$ has Laplace transform $e^{\|z\|^2/(4s)}$, mean $0$ and
  $\int\langle v,y\rangle^2d\gamma_s=\|v\|^2/(2s)$. For $\mu$ with finite second moment,
  $\mu*\gamma_s$ keeps the mean, has $\operatorname{Cov}(\mu*\gamma_s)=\operatorname{Cov}\mu+\tfrac1{2s}\mathrm{Id}$
  and $\int\|y\|^2$ increased by $n/(2s)$; these converge to the data of $\mu$ as $s\to\infty$.
  For $\mu$ with a Borell density, $\mu*\gamma_{n+1}\to\mu$ weakly.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Complete the square for the Laplace transform; differentiate at $0$ for the moments;
  independence of the summands for the convolution; $L^1$ convergence of the normalized
  densities for the weak limit.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Regular laws reduce to isotropic regular laws]
  \label{lem:bkl-regular-isotropic}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.covariance_pos,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.support_law,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.isotropic_comp_affine_of_whitening,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.exists_isotropic_comp_affine,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.exists_regular_isotropic}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-regular-affine}
  Section~2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~5 (reduction of Theorem~1.1 to isotropic regular
  laws). A regular law has finite second moment, full support and positive definite covariance,
  so a self-adjoint whitening $W$ exists; the affine potential $V(W^{-1}\cdot+m)$, $m$ the mean,
  is regular with positive constants and its law is isotropic, equal to the translated and
  whitened law. The whitening is not asserted to be unique or continuous in $\mu$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Zero variance of a nonzero linear form would make it constant a.e.\ for Lebesgue measure;
  then Lemma~\ref{lem:bkl-regular-affine} and the covariance transport rules.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Covariance of the quadratic tilt]
  \label{lem:bkl-quadratic-covariance}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Probability.memLp_id_two_quadraticTilt,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.inner_covariance_quadraticTilt_tendsto,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.covariance_quadraticTilt_tendsto}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-quadratic-tilt}
  Section~2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~5. For a probability law $\mu$ with finite second
  moment on a finite-dimensional inner product space, the quadratic tilts
  $\mu_\delta=\mu$ tilted by $e^{-\delta\|x\|^2/2}$ have finite second moments and
  $\operatorname{Cov}(\mu_\delta)\to\operatorname{Cov}(\mu)$ in operator norm as $\delta\to0^+$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The tilt weights are bounded by one; means and raw second moments converge by dominated
  convergence, hence every matrix entry, hence the operator in finite dimension.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Regular potentials under linear isometries]
  \label{lem:bkl-regular-isometry}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.bilinearIteratedFDerivTwo_comp_clm,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.comp_isometry,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.law_comp_isometry_eq_map,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.isotropic_comp_isometry,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.localSlope_constant_comp_isometry,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.toEuclidean,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.localSlope_le_of_euclidean_bound}
  \leanok
  Section~2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~5. For a linear isometry $L:E\to F$ between
  finite-dimensional inner product spaces, $V\circ L^{-1}$ is regular with the same constants
  $a,b$, its law is the image of the law of $V$ under $L$, isotropy and the smooth-test and
  literature Poincaré constants are preserved. Standard coordinates give a regular potential on
  $\mathbb R^n$, so a Poincaré bound for isotropic regular laws on $\mathbb R^n$ ($n>0$) transfers
  to every finite-dimensional inner product space (dimension zero has constant $0$). No such
  bound is asserted, and no non-isometric invariance is claimed.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Hessian chain rule for linear maps and norm preservation; the tilted law transports through
  the measure-preserving isometry.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The joint regular approximation]
  \label{lem:bkl-regular-approximation}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.exists_metric_diagonal,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.gaussian_joint_tendsto,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.row_joint_tendsto,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.exists_diagonal_limits,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.exists_regular_approximation_limits,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.GaussianSmoothing.exists_regularSeq_limits}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-regular-producer, lem:bkl-gaussian-moments, lem:bkl-quadratic-covariance}
  Section~2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~5. Let $\mu$ have a Borell density and finite
  second moment. The two-step laws (Gaussian smoothing of variance $1/(2(n+1))$, then the
  quadratic tilt of rate $\delta_k=1/(k+1)$) are the laws of regular potentials with
  $\delta_k\le\nabla^2\le2(n+1)+\delta_k$; along a diagonal $k(n)\ge n$ they converge weakly to
  $\mu$, with means, raw second moments and covariance operators converging to those of $\mu$.
  No convergence of whitened laws is asserted.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Each row converges to its smoothing as $k\to\infty$ and the smoothings converge to $\mu$; select
  the diagonal in the metrizable product of the weak topology with the statistics.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Conditional Poincaré transfer to regular laws]
  \label{lem:bkl-regular-poincare-transfer}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.localSlope_le_covariance_norm,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.localSlope_le_covariance_envelope,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.envelope_coefficient_tendsto}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-regular-isotropic}
  Section~2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~5. Suppose every isotropic regular law with
  positive upper curvature has literature Poincaré constant at most $K$ (an explicit hypothesis
  of this transfer lemma, discharged separately in Theorem~\ref{thm:bkl-poincare}). Then every regular law $\mu$ has
  $C_P(\mu)\le K\|\operatorname{Cov}\mu\|$, and $C_P(\mu)\le K(1+\|\operatorname{Cov}\mu-\mathrm{Id}\|)$,
  a coefficient converging to $K$ along laws whose covariances converge to $\mathrm{Id}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Center and whiten to an isotropic regular law (Lemma~\ref{lem:bkl-regular-isotropic}); the
  covariance-energy constant is invariant under this change of variables, and the Lipschitz
  energy is bounded by $\|\operatorname{Cov}\|$ times the covariance energy.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Letwin: mean Hessian of a convex potential]
  \label{lem:letwin-mean-hessian}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.integrable_of_cutoff_bounds,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integrable_hessian_diag_tilted_of_convexOn,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integrable_hessian_tilted_of_convexOn,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_hessian_tilted_eq_integral_fderiv_mul_of_convexOn,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.integral_hessian_eq_inner_of_isotropic_map}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:letwin-support-identities}
  Letwin \cite{Letwin2026QuadraticKLS}, Lemma~2.3, analytic part for a given potential. Let
  $\varphi$ be smooth and convex with $e^{-\varphi}$ a probability density on a finite-dimensional
  inner product space, $\nu=e^{-\varphi}\,dx$, and $\nabla\varphi\in L^2(\nu)$. Then every Hessian
  entry $\partial_u\partial_v\varphi$ is $\nu$-integrable and
  $\int\partial_u\partial_v\varphi\,d\nu=\int\partial_u\varphi\,\partial_v\varphi\,d\nu$; if
  $(\nabla\varphi)_\#\nu$ is isotropic, this equals $\langle u,v\rangle$ (and
  $\nabla\varphi\in L^2$ follows from isotropy). The moment map $\varphi$ of Lemma~2.2 for a given
  law is not constructed.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Integration by parts (A.3) with $f=\partial_v\varphi$ against the cutoffs $\chi_R$ gives
  $\int\chi_R\partial_v^2\varphi=\int\chi_R(\partial_v\varphi)^2-\int\partial_v\chi_R\,\partial_v\varphi$,
  bounded uniformly in $R\ge1$; the diagonal entries are nonnegative by convexity, so Fatou gives
  integrability; polarization gives the mixed entries; the identity is A.3 with $g=1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Letwin: the Hessian--Stein kernel of a given potential]
  \label{lem:letwin-hessian-kernel}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Stein.hessianKernel,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Stein.chain_coordinate,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Stein.hessianKernel_quadratic_nonneg,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Stein.exists_measurable_leftInverse_gradient_of_injective,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.hessianKernel_compact_identity,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.hessianKernel_isSteinKernel,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.integral_hessianKernel_eq_one,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.exists_hessianSteinKernel_of_injective_gradient}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:letwin-stein-dual, lem:letwin-mean-hessian}
  Letwin \cite{Letwin2026QuadraticKLS}, Lemma~2.7, analytic part for a given potential. Let
  $\varphi$ be smooth and convex on $\mathbb R^n$ with $e^{-\varphi}$ a probability density,
  $\nu=e^{-\varphi}dx$, $(\nabla\varphi)_\#\nu=\mu$ isotropic, and $\nabla\varphi$ injective (an
  explicit prerequisite). With a measurable left inverse $\Psi$ of $\nabla\varphi$, the kernel
  $\tau=\nabla^2\varphi\circ\Psi$ is a Stein kernel of $\mu$ (Definition~2.6) with integrable,
  symmetric, positive semidefinite entries, mean $\mathrm{Id}$, and $\tau(\nabla\varphi\,y)=\nabla^2\varphi(y)$.
  The pullback $f\circ\nabla\varphi$ of a compact test is bounded, not assumed compactly
  supported. Not provided: the moment map of an arbitrary law, strict positive definiteness,
  the Hilbert--Schmidt $L^2$ bound (the rest of Lemma~2.7 and Theorem~2.5).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Chain rule $\partial_i(f\circ\nabla\varphi)=\sum_j\partial_{ij}\varphi\,(\partial_jf)\circ\nabla\varphi$;
  A.3 for the bounded pullback with all integrability from Lemma~\ref{lem:letwin-mean-hessian};
  transport through $(\nabla\varphi)_\#\nu=\mu$ with the a.e.\ coupling; symmetry and positive
  semidefiniteness of the Hessian; the mean is the mean-Hessian identity.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Nondivergence operator and the diffusion product rule]
  \label{lem:letwin-diffusion-product-rule}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.bilinearIteratedFDerivTwo_mul,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.nondivergenceOperator,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.nondivergenceOperator_mul}
  \leanok
  Letwin \cite{Letwin2026QuadraticKLS}, p.~6, display~(2.3): for a second-order operator in
  nondivergence form $L_{A,b}f=\sum_{ij}A_{ij}\,\partial_{ij}f-\sum_i b_i\,\partial_if$ with an arbitrary
  real coefficient field $A$ (symmetric at each point) and drift $b$, and $C^2$ functions $f,g$,
  $L_{A,b}(fg)=f\,L_{A,b}g+g\,L_{A,b}f+2\sum_{ij}A_{ij}\,\partial_if\,\partial_jg$; the underlying Hessian
  product rule $D^2(fg)(u,v)=f\,D^2g(u,v)+g\,D^2f(u,v)+Df(u)Dg(v)+Dg(u)Df(v)$ is generic.  The source
  specialization $A=(\nabla^2\varphi)^{-1}$ (the moment-map metric), the Monge--Amp\`ere and Brascamp--Lieb
  inputs and Theorem~2.5 are not provided.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Hessian product rule in the standard orthonormal basis; the three bilinear sums regroup into the
  two operator terms and the carr\'e-du-champ term.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Inverse Hessian derivative and the inverse-Hessian operator]
  \label{lem:letwin-inverse-hessian-derivative}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.fderiv_ringInverse_comp_apply,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.operatorEntry_ringInverse_fderiv_apply,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.hessianOperator,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.hessianOperator_isUnit_of_posDef,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.inner_ringInverse_hessianOperator_pos,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.fderiv_ringInverse_hessianOperator_apply,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.fderiv_inverseHessianEntry_apply,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.hessianInverseOperator_mul,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.fderiv_logDetHessian_of_mongeAmpere,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.contDiffAt_comp_gradient_of_mem}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:letwin-diffusion-product-rule}
  Letwin \cite{Letwin2026QuadraticKLS}, pp.~5--7, (2.5) and the (2.3) operator with the inverse
  Hessian, for a given smooth $\varphi$ with positive definite Hessian.  The Hessian operator
  $\nabla^2\varphi(x)$ is a self-adjoint unit with positive definite inverse; the inverse Hessian is
  symmetric and inverts the Hessian in orthonormal coordinates; its derivative is
  $\partial_k(\nabla^2\varphi)^{-1}_{ij}=-\sum_{a,c}(\nabla^2\varphi)^{-1}_{ia}\,\partial_{kac}\varphi\,(\nabla^2\varphi)^{-1}_{cj}$
  (operator and coordinate forms, from the derivative $-H^{-1}(DH)H^{-1}$ of an operator inverse).
  The operator $L=\sum_{ij}(\nabla^2\varphi)^{-1}_{ij}\partial_{ij}-\sum_i(\partial_iV\circ\nabla\varphi)\partial_i$
  satisfies the Leibniz rule.  If $\log\det\nabla^2\varphi=-\varphi+V\circ\nabla\varphi$ holds (the zero-order
  Monge--Amp\`ere equation~(2.2), a hypothesis), with $V$ smooth on an open set containing the gradient
  image, then $D(\log\det\nabla^2\varphi)[v]=-D\varphi[v]+DV(\nabla\varphi)[\nabla^2\varphi\,v]$.  Not
  provided: Jacobi's derivative of the determinant, Lemma~2.4, Lemma~2.6, the positive-semidefinite
  estimates, Brascamp--Lieb, and the existence of the moment map (Lemma~2.2).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Mathlib's derivative of the inverse in a normed algebra composed with the Hessian field; symmetry
  of the Hessian; invertibility from positive definiteness; coordinates in an orthonormal basis; the
  nondivergence Leibniz rule with the symmetric inverse-Hessian coefficient; the chain rule for
  $V\circ\nabla\varphi$ on the open neighbourhood.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Jacobi's formula and the first Monge--Amp\`ere derivative]
  \label{lem:letwin-monge-ampere-first}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.fderiv_det_eq_det_mul_trace,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.fderiv_logDet_comp_apply,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.fderiv_logDetHessian_eq_trace,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.fderiv_logDetHessian_eq_sum,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.mongeAmpere_first_derivative,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.mongeAmpere_first_derivative_on_range}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:letwin-inverse-hessian-derivative}
  Jacobi's formula $D(\det)(M)[N]=\det M\,\mathrm{tr}(M^{-1}N)$ and $D(\log\det)(M)[N]=\mathrm{tr}(M^{-1}N)$ for
  real square matrices $M$ with $\det M\ne0$ (the determinant formula assumes $\det M$ is a unit, i.e.\ $M$ invertible), and the
  $\log\det$ formula along a differentiable matrix field $x\mapsto M(x)$ at points with $\det M(x)\ne0$. (The permutation formula of
  Lemma~\ref{lem:determinant-permutation-differential} has no such restriction and covers singular matrices.)  For a smooth $\varphi$ with positive
  definite Hessian, $D(\log\det\nabla^2\varphi)[v]=\sum_{a,c}(\nabla^2\varphi)^{-1}_{ac}\,\partial_v\partial_{ac}\varphi$.
  Hence Letwin \cite{Letwin2026QuadraticKLS}, display~(2.4): if $\log\det\nabla^2\varphi=-\varphi+V\circ\nabla\varphi$
  (the zero-order Monge--Amp\`ere equation~(2.2), a hypothesis) with $V$ smooth on an open neighbourhood of
  $\overline K\supseteq\overline{\mathrm{range}\,\nabla\varphi}$, then
  $\sum_{a,c}(\nabla^2\varphi)^{-1}_{ac}\partial_{iac}\varphi=-\partial_i\varphi+\sum_a\partial_aV(\nabla\varphi)\,\partial_{ai}\varphi$.
  The moment map itself (Lemma~2.2) is not constructed.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The determinant is a continuous multilinear map of the rows; its derivative is the sum of row
  replacements, which the inverse turns into $\det M\,\mathrm{tr}(M^{-1}N)$.  Compose with the smooth
  Hessian field and identify the inverse matrix with the inverse-Hessian entries; the right-hand side
  is Lemma~\ref{lem:letwin-inverse-hessian-derivative}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Second Monge--Amp\`ere derivative and its positive semidefinite terms]
  \label{lem:letwin-monge-ampere-second}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.bilinearIteratedFDerivTwo_bilinearIteratedFDerivTwo_comm,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.bilinearIteratedFDerivTwo_self_nonneg_of_convexOn_open,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.posSemidef_weightedGram,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.mongeAmpere_second_derivative_coordinate,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.mongeAmpere_second_derivative,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.mongeAmpere_second_derivative_on_range,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.posSemidef_hessian_conj_hessian_on_range,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.posSemidef_inverseHessian_third_gram}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:letwin-monge-ampere-first}
  Letwin \cite{Letwin2026QuadraticKLS}, display~(2.6): differentiating~(2.4) once more, with the
  inverse-Hessian operator $L$ of~(2.3),
  $L(\partial_{ij}\varphi)+\partial_{ij}\varphi=\big(\nabla^2\varphi\,\nabla^2V(\nabla\varphi)\,\nabla^2\varphi\big)_{ij}
  +\sum_{a,c,p,q}(\nabla^2\varphi)^{-1}_{ap}(\nabla^2\varphi)^{-1}_{cq}\,\partial_{iac}\varphi\,\partial_{jpq}\varphi$,
  under the zero-order equation, $\varphi\in C^\infty$ with positive definite Hessian, and $V$ smooth on a
  neighbourhood of $\overline K$.  The commutation of third and fourth derivatives is derived from $C^2$
  symmetry.  Both right-hand matrices are positive semidefinite: the first when $V$ is convex on the open
  set $K$ containing the gradient image (no convexity on the larger neighbourhood is required), the second
  as a Gram matrix weighted by $(\nabla^2\varphi)^{-1}\otimes(\nabla^2\varphi)^{-1}$.  Not provided: the
  moment map, Theorem~2.5, Brascamp--Lieb, Proposition~2.8, any Hilbert--Schmidt or operator-norm bound,
  and the principal root.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Differentiate both sides of~(2.4) in the direction $e_j$: the product rule on the left with the
  inverse-Hessian derivative of Lemma~\ref{lem:letwin-inverse-hessian-derivative}, the chain rule for
  $\partial_aV\circ\nabla\varphi$ on the right; regroup with the commutation lemmas.  Positivity: a $C^2$
  function convex on an open set has nonnegative Hessian, conjugation by the symmetric Hessian preserves
  positive semidefiniteness, and $F(A\otimes A)F^{\!\top}$ is positive semidefinite.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Integration by parts for the inverse-Hessian operator on compact tests]
  \label{lem:letwin-inverse-hessian-ibp}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.inverseHessian_divergence,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.density_inverseHessian_divergence,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.density_hessianInverseOperator_flux,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_fderiv_mul_eq_neg_of_hasCompactSupport,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_hessianInverseOperator_mul_eq_neg,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_hessianInverseOperator_mul_eq_neg_on_range,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_hessianInverseOperator_eq_zero,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_hessianInverseOperator_mul_comm}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:letwin-monge-ampere-first}
  Letwin \cite{Letwin2026QuadraticKLS}, display~(2.2): for a given smooth $\varphi$ with everywhere
  positive definite Hessian, $\int e^{-\varphi}=1$ and the zero-order equation
  $\log\det\nabla^2\varphi=-\varphi+V\circ\nabla\varphi$ with $V$ smooth on an open set containing
  $\nabla\varphi(\mathbb R^n)$ (in the source, a neighbourhood of $\overline K$), the inverse Hessian
  $A=(\nabla^2\varphi)^{-1}$ has coordinate divergence $\sum_i\partial_iA_{ij}=\sum_iA_{ij}\partial_i\varphi
  -\partial_jV(\nabla\varphi)$, so $\sum_i\partial_i(e^{-\varphi}A_{ij})=-e^{-\varphi}\partial_jV(\nabla\varphi)$
  and $\sum_{ij}\partial_i(e^{-\varphi}A_{ij}\partial_jf)=e^{-\varphi}Lf$; hence for smooth $f,g$ with one of
  them compactly supported, $\int(Lf)\,g\,d\nu=-\int\sum_{ij}A_{ij}\partial_if\,\partial_jg\,d\nu$ with
  $d\nu=e^{-\varphi}dx$, $\int Lf\,d\nu=0$, and $\int(Lf)g\,d\nu=\int f(Lg)\,d\nu$ on compact tests.  Haar
  integrability comes from the compact test alone.  The noncompact extension is
  Lemmas~\ref{lem:letwin-a4-cutoffs} and~\ref{lem:letwin-a5-cutoffs}.  Not provided: the moment map of an arbitrary law,
  a closed $L^2$ generator, Brascamp--Lieb, Theorem~2.5, Proposition~2.8.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Multiply the first derivative~(2.4) of the equation by the inverse Hessian and use the third-slot
  commutation; the product rule with $e^{-\varphi}$ gives the flux identities; directional Haar
  integration by parts on each coordinate term, with integrability from compact support, and the tilt
  normalisation.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Parameter response of the localization posterior]
  \label{lem:bkl-posterior-parameter-response}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Posterior.quadraticExponent,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Posterior.expectation_hasDerivAt_of_compact,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Posterior.expectation_hasDerivAt_quadraticExponent_of_coercive,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Posterior.expectation_hasDerivAt_quadraticExponent_of_compact,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Posterior.exists_uniform_norm_moments_tilted_quadraticExponent_of_compact,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isotropic.posterior_linear_response}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-weak-limit-transfer}
  \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~19, the compact stage before~(67) and the deterministic parameter
  dependence of the posterior $\mu_{c,Q}=\mu\,e^{\langle c,x\rangle-\frac12\langle Qx,x\rangle}/Z$ of~(67).
  For a compactly supported prior, every parameter pair (including $c=Q=0$) gives a probability
  posterior, continuous statistics are in $L^2$, all norm moments are bounded uniformly in $(c,Q)$ by a
  support radius, and for $f$ bounded on the support
  $\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\int f\,d\mu_{c+th,\,Q+tH}=\mathrm{Cov}_{\mu_{c,Q}}\big(f,\langle h,x\rangle-\tfrac12\langle Hx,x\rangle\big)$.
  For an arbitrary probability prior and $\langle Qx,x\rangle\ge a|x|^2$ with $a>0$, the same response holds
  for measurable $f$ of polynomial growth without any prior moment ($Q=0$ is not coercive in positive dimension, vacuously so in
  dimension zero, and is covered only by the compact branch).  For a compactly supported isotropic prior at $c=Q=0$ the response of
  $\langle u,x\rangle$ to the linear parameter in direction $h$ is $\langle u,h\rangle$.  The stochastic
  equations~(67)--(71), the It\^o formula, the martingale property and the compact approximation of
  general laws are not provided.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Domination of the parameter family on a neighbourhood (support bound, or the quadratic damping
  $e^{-\frac a4|x|^2}$ absorbing polynomial weights), differentiation under the integral, and the quotient
  rule for the normalized expectation; isotropy gives the covariance pairing at the initial pair.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Covariance domain of the localization posterior]
  \label{lem:bkl-posterior-covariance-domain}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Posterior.compactPosterior,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Posterior.compactPosterior_affineSupport,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Posterior.compactPosterior_covariance_pos_of_affineSupport_eq_top,
        FunctionalInequalities.Covariance.inner_operator_self_pos_of_affineSupport_eq_top,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.affineSupport_eq_top_of_covariance_injective,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isotropic.compactPosterior_exists_positive_symmetric_inverse,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isotropic.compactPosterior_ringInverse_covariance,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isotropic.compactPosterior_generator_cancellation,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isotropic.ringInverse_covariance_compactPosterior_zero,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.logConcave_of_concave_tilt,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.compactPosterior_logConcave,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.inverseSqrt_spec,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.inverseSqrt_unique,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.inverseSqrtIn_eq_of_basis,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isotropic.compactPosterior_inverseSqrt_spec,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isotropic.inverseSqrt_covariance_compactPosterior_zero}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-posterior-parameter-response}
  \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, (67)--(68): for a compactly supported prior $\mu$ and every finite
  parameter pair $(c,Q)$ (including $c=Q=0$ and negative $Q$) the posterior
  $\mu_{c,Q}=\mu\,e^{\langle c,x\rangle-\frac12\langle Qx,x\rangle}/Z$ is a probability law with the null
  sets, support and affine support of $\mu$ and finite second moment.  A law with finite second moment and full affine support has
  strictly positive covariance, and an injective covariance forces full affine support; hence for an
  isotropic compact prior every $\mu_{c,Q}$ has a self-adjoint, strictly positive, invertible covariance,
  whose canonical inverse $\mathrm{Cov}(\mu_{c,Q})^{-1}$ is self-adjoint and strictly positive (the rate
  $A_t^{-1}$ in the parameter equations~(67); (68) is the equation of the expectations).  A positive-dimensional Dirac prior shows that compactness alone does not give a unit.
  Not provided: a principal inverse square root or whitener, coefficient regularity, positivity of the
  $Q$-process, the stochastic equations and It\^o calculus, and any Theorem~4.1 or main-theorem claim.
  The diffusion coefficient $A^{-1/2}$ of~(67) is the principal inverse square root of the covariance,
  obtained from the canonical matrix square root in orthonormal coordinates: positive, self-adjoint,
  $\sigma^2=\sigma\sigma^*=A^{-1}$, $A\sigma\sigma^*=\sigma\sigma^*A=\mathrm{Id}$, the unique positive
  operator with that square, basis independent, equal to $\mathrm{Id}$ at $c=Q=0$; coefficient algebra only
  (no continuity or Lipschitz property of $(c,Q)\mapsto A^{-1/2}$, no whitener, no stochastic statement).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Mathlib absolute continuity of normalized tilts in both directions; the hyperplane argument for a
  vanishing variance against full affine support; kernel of the covariance versus the direction of the
  affine support; a strictly positive operator is a unit, and right inverses of a self-adjoint
  strictly positive unit are self-adjoint, strictly positive and equal to \texttt{Ring.inverse}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Coefficient regularity of the localization equations]
  \label{lem:bkl-coefficients-regularity}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Posterior.contDiff_posteriorExpectation_joint,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Posterior.contDiff_posteriorMean_joint,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Posterior.contDiff_posteriorCovariance_joint,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isotropic.contDiff_ringInverse_covariance_joint,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isotropic.contDiff_covarianceDrift_joint,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.root_difference_le_div,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.locallyLipschitz_matrixEntries_sqrt,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.locallyLipschitz_inverseSqrt_of_contDiff_ringInverse,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isotropic.locallyLipschitz_inverseSqrt_covariance_joint,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isotropic.locallyLipschitz_coefficients67}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-posterior-covariance-domain}
  \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, (67), compact stage: for a compactly supported prior $\mu$ the posterior
  expectation $(c,Q)\mapsto\int f\,d\mu_{c,Q}$ of a measurable $f$ bounded on the support is $C^\infty$
  jointly in $(c,Q)\in E\times\mathcal L(E)$ (operator norm on the second factor; every finite pair,
  including $Q=0$, non-symmetric $Q$ and dimension zero), hence so are the mean $a(c,Q)$ and the covariance
  $A(c,Q)$; for an isotropic prior the rate $A^{-1}=\mathrm{Cov}(\mu_{c,Q})^{-1}$ and the drift $A^{-1}a$
  are jointly $C^\infty$, and the diffusion coefficient $A^{-1/2}$ (principal inverse square root) and the
  coefficient triple $(A^{-1/2},A^{-1}a,A^{-1})$ are locally Lipschitz on the whole parameter space.  The
  root estimate is the Frobenius bound $\|S-T\|_F\le\|S^2-T^2\|_F/(a+b)$ for positive floors $a,b$ and
  symmetric $T$ (from $S(S-T)+(S-T)T=S^2-T^2$, no commutation), with continuity of the canonical matrix
  square root on the nonnegative cone and a local positive floor.  Not provided: a strong solution of (67),
  non-explosion, adaptedness, It\^o calculus, or any Theorem~4.1 claim; nothing is claimed about the root
  off the self-adjoint positive cone.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The exponent $\langle c,x\rangle-\tfrac12\langle Qx,x\rangle$ is continuous linear in $(c,Q)$; after a
  finite-dimensional chart it is the pairing with a Riesz feature $\psi(x)$, so the joint expectation is
  the $c$-only expectation of the pushed-forward prior $\psi_*\mu$ (compactly supported) at $Q'=0$, which
  is $C^\infty$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:bkl-posterior-covariance-domain}'s generator calculus, composed with the
  chart.  Mean and covariance are finite sums of such expectations times rank-one operators; the inverse
  is analytic on units; local Lipschitzness of the root follows from the Sylvester estimate on a
  neighbourhood with a common floor, transported to the operator norm by the finite-dimensional
  coordinate charts.
\end{proof}

  \label{lem:bkl-posterior-generator}

  \uses{lem:bkl-posterior-parameter-response}
  \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, (67)--(68), compact-prior stage, deterministic part.  For a
  compactly supported prior and a measurable $f$ bounded on the support, the posterior expectation
  $(c,Q)\mapsto\int f\,d\mu_{c,Q}$ is $C^\infty$ in $c$ with $c$-gradient $\mathrm{Cov}(f,\langle h,x\rangle)$,
  $c$-Hessian $\mathrm{Cov}(f,\langle h,x\rangle\langle k,x\rangle)-\langle h,a\rangle\mathrm{Cov}(f,\langle k,x\rangle)
  -\langle k,a\rangle\mathrm{Cov}(f,\langle h,x\rangle)$ ($a$ the posterior mean), and $Q$-derivative
  $-\tfrac12\mathrm{Cov}(f,\langle Mx,x\rangle)$ in a direction $M$; the tilted covariance is differentiable
  along exponent lines.  For every symmetric $M$ the generator combination
  $D_c[Ma]+\partial_Q[M]+\tfrac12\sum_i D^2_c[Me_i,e_i]$ vanishes, in every orthonormal basis.
  With $M=A_t^{-1}$ this is the deterministic content of the zero drift in~(68); the stochastic
  equation~(67), It\^o's formula and the martingale property are not constructed, and the
  specialization to the inverse covariance is stated separately.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Smoothness of weighted integrals inside every exponential radius (compact support gives all
  radii), the quotient rule for the normalization, identification of the directional responses with
  the Fr\'echet derivatives, the trace contraction of the Hessian against $M$, and cancellation of
  the three covariance terms.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Conditional reduction of Theorem 1.1 for regular isotropic laws]
  \label{lem:bkl-regular-reduction}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.taylorTensor_one_apply,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isotropic.norm_coordTensor_taylorTensor_one_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.two_mul_pow_le_two_mul_pow,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.poincare_le_of_cumulantBound}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:bkl-taylor-criterion, prop:bkl-suspension}
  Proof of Theorem~1.1 in \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~30, as a conditional statement.
  Suppose the cumulant bounds of Theorem~4.1 hold universally: $R^\nu_{d+1}\le(d!)^2K^d\,\mathrm{Id}$
  for every isotropic log-concave $\nu$ in every dimension and every $d\ge2$, with $K\ge1$. Then
  every isotropic regular law $\mu$ on $\mathbb R^n$ satisfies $C_P(\mu)\le2CK$, where $C$ is the
  constant of Theorem~3.1. The hypothesis is not discharged in this lemma; Theorem~\ref{thm:bkl-poincare} discharges it.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  For $d\ge2$, Proposition~\ref{prop:bkl-suspension} with $b=(d!)^2K^d$ gives
  $|T_df|^2\le2K^d\|f\|^2\le(2K)^d\|f\|^2$. For $d=1$, $T_1f(e_j)=\int x_jf\,d\mu$ (the first
  Taylor tensor is the centered first moment) and Bessel's inequality under isotropy gives
  $|T_1f|\le\|f\|$. Hence~(29) holds with $R=\sqrt{2K}\ge1$, and
  Theorem~\ref{thm:bkl-taylor-criterion} gives $C_P(\mu)\le CR^2=2CK$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Reduction to isotropic regular laws, and Theorem 1.1 conditional on Theorem 4.1]
  \label{lem:bkl-isotropic-reduction}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.exists_isBorellDensity_of_isotropic,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.localSlope_le_of_regular_isotropic_bound,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.localSlope_le_of_euclidean_regular_isotropic_bound,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.isotropicBound_of_euclidean_regular_isotropic_bound,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.localSlope_le_of_cumulantBound_all,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.conjecture_of_cumulantBound}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-regular-approximation, lem:bkl-regular-poincare-transfer, lem:bkl-regular-isometry,
        lem:bkl-weak-limit-transfer, lem:bkl-regular-reduction}
  Section~2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}, p.~5, and the proof of Theorem~1.1 on p.~30. If every
  isotropic regular law on $\mathbb R^n$, $n>0$, has literature Poincaré constant at most $K$, then
  every isotropic log-concave law in every finite-dimensional inner product space has constant at
  most $K$: such a law has a Borell density, is the weak limit of laws of regular potentials with
  covariances tending to $\mathrm{Id}$, the bound transfers with the coefficient
  $K(1+\|\operatorname{Cov}-\mathrm{Id}\|)\to K$, and the weak-limit passage concludes. Combined with
  the p.~30 reduction: under the universal cumulant bounds of Theorem~4.1, every isotropic
  log-concave law has $C_P\le2CK$, and the project's KLS conjecture follows from that hypothesis.
  This lemma keeps Theorem~4.1 as a hypothesis; Theorem~\ref{thm:bkl-poincare} discharges it, and
  Conjecture~\ref{conj:kls} follows.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Isotropy forces full affine support, hence a Borell density; apply
  Lemmas~\ref{lem:bkl-regular-approximation}, \ref{lem:bkl-regular-poincare-transfer},
  \ref{lem:bkl-regular-isometry} and \ref{lem:bkl-weak-limit-transfer}; then
  Lemma~\ref{lem:bkl-regular-reduction}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Letwin: Stein kernels, the $H^{-1}$ norm, A.7 and Lemma 2.9]
  \label{lem:letwin-stein-dual}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Stein.IsSteinKernel,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Stein.hsNorm,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Stein.compact_directional_identity,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Stein.extended_identity_of_ac,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Stein.extended_abs_bound_of_ac,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.Dual.UnitEnergyTest,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.Dual.dualNorm,
        FunctionalInequalities.Energy.Dual.toReal_dualNorm_eq_signed_sSup,
        FunctionalInequalities.Probability.Stein.linear_dual_bound_of_ac,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.stein_identity_of_affineSupport_eq_top,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.dualNorm_linear_sq_le_of_affineSupport_eq_top}
  \leanok
  Letwin \cite{Letwin2026QuadraticKLS}, Definition~2.6, (2.19), Appendix~A.7 and Lemma~2.9 (supporting the
  Letwin bound used in BKL Lemma~4.2). A Stein kernel of a centered law $\mu$ on $\mathbb R^n$ is a
  matrix field $\tau$ with $\int x_if\,d\mu=\int(\tau\nabla f)_i\,d\mu$ for $f\in C_c^\infty$; with
  a.e.\ symmetry and $|\tau|_{HS}\in L^2(\mu)$, for $\mu\ll\mathrm{vol}$ with finite second moment the
  identity $\int\langle x,v\rangle f=\int\langle\tau v,\nabla f\rangle$ extends to all locally
  Lipschitz $f$ with $f,\nabla f\in L^2(\mu)$ (A.7), and
  $\|\langle\cdot,v\rangle\|_{H^{-1}(\mu)}^2\le\int\|\tau(x)v\|^2\,d\mu$ (Lemma~2.9), where
  $\|q\|_{H^{-1}}=\sup\{\int qf\,d\mu:\int\|\nabla f\|^2\,d\mu\le1\}$ is taken as an extended real
  over the full unit-energy class and identified with the real signed supremum once finite. Full
  affine dimension and log-concavity supply absolute continuity and the second moment. No Stein
  kernel is constructed here; the moment-map kernel is Lemma~\ref{lem:convex-source-smooth-stein-kernel}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Compact tests: sum the coordinate identities and use symmetry. Extension: approximate $f$ by
  $C_c^\infty$ functions in the graph norm and pass both pairings to the limit in $L^2$.
  Lemma~2.9: A.7 and Cauchy--Schwarz bound every unit-energy pairing by
  $(\int\|\tau v\|^2)^{1/2}$; finiteness then identifies the extended value with the real supremum.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Regular laws]
  \label{lem:bkl-regular-law}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.isProbabilityMeasure,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.integrable_exp_mul_norm,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.exists_norm_gradient_le,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.logConcave_law,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.taylorTensor_neg_weightedLaplacianVec,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.integral_hessian_add_le_norm_sq_weightedLaplacianVec,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.existsUnique_centered_adjointComp_eq,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.RegularPotential.existsUnique_centered_adjointComp_eq_components}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-tilt-generator,lem:bkl-bochner-temperate,lem:bkl-centered-inverse}
  The regularity assumption~(12) of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}: $V$ is smooth with all derivatives
  of polynomial growth and $a\le\nabla^2V\le b$ with $a>0$ (the source's $b>0$ follows in positive
  dimension; in dimension $0$ any $b$ is allowed).  The law is the probability measure
  $\mathrm{volume}.\mathrm{tilted}(-V)$, of density $e^{-V}/Z$ with $Z=\int e^{-V}dx$; the source's
  normalized potential is $V+\log Z$.  From~(12) alone it follows that $V$ grows at least quadratically, $e^{-V}$
  is integrable with $Z>0$, the law is a log-concave probability measure with full-dimensional support,
  it has all exponential moments $\int e^{r|x|}$, and $\nabla V$ has polynomial growth.  Consequently
  the tensor-valued Lemma~3.6, the curvature form of~(16) with constant $a$, and the operator-domain
  part of Lemma~2.1 with $\norm u\le a^{-1}\norm g$ (also componentwise for tensor data, with
  $\sum_k\norm{u_k}^2\le a^{-2}\norm g^2$) hold for every regular law with no further analytic
  premise (the test fields remain of temperate growth and the data $g\in L^2$ centered).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Taylor's formula with the Hessian lower bound gives $V(x)\ge V(0)+DV(0)x+\tfrac a2|x|^2$; Gaussian
  domination gives the integrability statements; the temperate-growth assumption bounds $\nabla V$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Energy recursion]
  \label{lem:bkl-energy-recursion}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.gradCoord,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_norm_sq_weightedLaplacianVec_eq_of_poisson,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_norm_sq_gradCoord_gradCoord_eq,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_norm_sq_gradCoord_gradCoord_eq_add,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_norm_sq_weightedLaplacianVec_ge,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.summable_and_tendsto_zero_of_energy,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.summable_and_tendsto_zero_of_poisson_chain,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_norm_sq_gradCoord_sub_eq_of_normalized,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_norm_sq_gradCoord_eq_add_sum,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.norm_sq_integral_gradCoord_eq,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.norm_sq_integral_gradient_le_of_eigen}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:bkl-ibp-temperate,lem:bkl-bochner-temperate,lem:bkl-tilt-generator}
  Partial counterpart of Lemma~3.4 and Corollary~3.5 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}: the
  generalized implication for supplied iterates.  Let $\mu=m.\mathrm{tilted}(-V)$ with $V$ of temperate growth, all exponential
  moments and $\nabla^2V\ge a$.  Gradients of tensor fields add a first index, with the
  Hilbert--Schmidt norm.  For tensor fields $g,h$ of temperate growth with $-\mathcal Lh=\alpha\nabla_0g$
  (relation~(30)) and $\int\norm{\nabla h}^2=\int\norm{\nabla_0g}^2$ (normalization~(33)), the
  identities~(39), (46), (47) hold and
  $\int\norm{\mathcal Lh}^2+\int\norm{\nabla^2g-\alpha\nabla h}^2+a\int\norm{\nabla g}^2\le\int\norm{\mathcal Lg}^2$
  (inequality~(45)).  If $a>0$, nonnegative sequences satisfying~(45) have summable energies tending
  to $0$; in particular this holds for every sequence of tensor iterates $h_k$ with values in spaces
  $G_k$ linked by linear isometries $\mathbb R^n\otimes G_k\cong G_{k+1}$ whose consecutive pairs
  satisfy~(30) and~(33).  Under~(33) alone, $v_i-v_{i+1}=\abs{\int\nabla h_i\,d\mu}^2$~(35) for a pair
  and $v_0=v_N+\sum_{k<N}\abs{\int\nabla h_k\,d\mu}^2$ along a sequence (the form of~(36); these use that
  $\mu$ is a probability measure).  For a pair satisfying~(30), Lemma~3.6 gives
  $\int\nabla h\,d\mu=\alpha\,T_1(\nabla_0g)$, hence $\abs{\int\nabla h}^2=\alpha^2\abs{T_1(\nabla_0g)}^2$ in
  Hilbert--Schmidt norms (the identity~(61) used in Lemma~3.9); and for a supplied centered
  eigenfunction $\mathcal Lf=-\lambda f$ and covariance at most $K\ge0$,
  $\abs{\int\nabla f}^2\le\lambda^2K\norm f_2^2$ (the bound on $p_1$ in the proof of Lemma~3.9).  The existence of the source's iterates (Lemma~2.1 on $\mathcal A$ and
  the first eigenfunction) is not proved.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Integration by parts~(15) for the pair, the tensor Bochner formula~(16) with the curvature bound,
  and telescoping.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Letwin supporting identities]
  \label{lem:letwin-support-identities}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.BakryEmery.integral_fderiv_mul_tilted_eq,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.Tensor.sum_weight_sq_mul_sq_sub_eq_half_sum_sq_sub_mul_sq}
  \leanok
  Two identities from Letwin, arXiv:2607.24164v1, used in the proof of its Theorem~2.5
  (dependency of Lemma~4.2 of \cite{BizeulKlartagLehec2026}). Lemma~A.3 (pp.~16--17): for smooth
  $\varphi,f,g$ with $\int e^{-\varphi}\,dm=1$ and $(\partial_vf)g$, $f\partial_vg$,
  $fg\,\partial_v\varphi$, $fg$ integrable under $\nu=e^{-\varphi}m$,
  $\int(\partial_vf)g\,d\nu=\int f\,((\partial_v\varphi)g-\partial_vg)\,d\nu$, for any direction $v$.
  Equation~(2.12) (p.~10), finite form: for an array $T$ symmetric in its first two indices and
  arbitrary real weights $b$,
  $\sum b_i^2T_{ijk}^2-\sum b_ib_jT_{ijk}^2=\tfrac12\sum(b_i-b_j)^2T_{ijk}^2\ge0$.
  These identities alone do not construct the moment potential or prove Theorem~2.5. The weak $C^{1,1}$ moment potential, its generator identities and its trace bound are developed below and used by Theorem~\ref{thm:letwin-compact-quadratic-bound}; the full smooth moment-map statement of Lemma~2.2 remains outside that scope.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Mathlib's directional integration by parts applied to $f$ and $g e^{-\varphi}$; a pointwise
  polynomial identity after swapping the first two indices.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Letwin: covariance of the transported vector]
  \label{lem:letwin-mapped-covariance}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.Isotropic.covarianceBilin_map_sqrt_abs_matrix,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.symmetric_matrix_sqrt_real_cfc_bridge,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.symmetric_matrix_sign_factorization}
  \leanok
  Letwin \cite{Letwin2026QuadraticKLS}, proof of Theorem~1.2, p.~14, the unnumbered covariance step preceding~(2.21), in
  a reviewed stronger form: for an isotropic probability law of $X$ on $\mathbb R^n$ and every real symmetric $M$
  (singular and indefinite included, $n=0$ allowed), with $|M|=\sqrt{M^2}$ and $Z=|M|^{1/2}X$, $\mathrm{Cov}(Z)=|M|$; the
  source first reduces to invertible $M$ (Lemma~A.2).  Matrix supports: $|M|$ and $|M|^{1/2}$ agree with the real
  continuous functional calculus and are positive semidefinite, $|M|^{1/2}\,\mathrm{sgn}(M)\,|M|^{1/2}=M$, and
  $\mathrm{sgn}(M)^2=\mathrm{Id}$ for invertible $M$.  Not provided: the Stein-kernel trace identity~(2.21), Theorem~2.5,
  Proposition~2.8, Lemma~2.9 for $F(z)=\langle\mathrm{sgn}(M)z,z\rangle-\mathrm{Tr}\,M$, and Theorem~1.2.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The covariance of a linear image $L_\#\mu$ is $L\,\mathrm{Cov}(\mu)\,L^{T}$, isotropy gives $\mathrm{Cov}(\mu)=\mathrm{Id}$,
  and $|M|^{1/2}|M|^{1/2}=|M|$ in the continuous functional calculus.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Letwin: Jordan decomposition and trace energy of symmetric matrices (Theorem~2.5 support)]
  \label{lem:letwin-symmetric-jordan}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.cfc_posSemidef_of_nonneg,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.trace_cross_nonneg,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.trace_energy_nonneg,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.jordan_trace_domination,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.symmetric_jordan_decomposition,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.symmetric_jordan_trace_support}
  \leanok
  Letwin \cite{Letwin2026QuadraticKLS}, Theorem~2.5, support for the extension from positive semidefinite to all
  symmetric $B$, for real matrices over any finite index type (the empty one included).  A nonnegative real function of a
  symmetric matrix is positive semidefinite.  Every symmetric $B$ is $P-N$ with $P=\mathrm{cfc}(\max(\cdot,0))\,B$ and
  $N=\mathrm{cfc}(\max(-\cdot,0))\,B$ positive semidefinite, $PN=NP=0$ and $\operatorname{tr}B^2=\operatorname{tr}P^2+
  \operatorname{tr}N^2$; no decomposition is assumed.  For positive semidefinite $P,N$ and symmetric $H$,
  $\operatorname{tr}(PHNH)\ge0$ and $\operatorname{tr}((P-N)H(P-N)H)\le\operatorname{tr}(PHPH)+\operatorname{tr}(NHNH)$,
  without commutation; and $\operatorname{tr}(BHBH)\ge0$ for symmetric $B$ and positive semidefinite $H$.  Not provided:
  the positive semidefinite integral bound and its measure extension, the moment map, Brascamp--Lieb, (2.12) for the
  inverse-Hessian contractions and Theorem~2.5 itself.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The continuous functional calculus acts on the eigenvalues in an orthonormal eigenbasis, so a nonnegative function
  gives a Gram matrix; the scalar identities $\max(x,0)-\max(-x,0)=x$ and $\max(x,0)\max(-x,0)=0$ pass through the
  calculus.  With $P=X^{\top}X$, $\operatorname{tr}(PHNH)=\operatorname{tr}((XH)N(XH)^{\top})\ge0$; with $H=X^{\top}X$,
  $\operatorname{tr}(BHBH)=\operatorname{tr}(C^2)$ for the symmetric $C=XBX^{\top}$; expanding $(P-N)H(P-N)H$ leaves the
  two nonnegative mixed traces.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Letwin: from positive semidefinite to symmetric matrices under a measure (Theorem~2.5 support)]
  \label{lem:letwin-symmetric-integral}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.symmetric_trace_integral_extension,
        FunctionalInequalities.Analysis.symmetric_trace_integral_extension_mul}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:letwin-symmetric-jordan}
  Letwin \cite{Letwin2026QuadraticKLS}, Theorem~2.5, the extension step, conditional on the positive semidefinite bound:
  for an arbitrary measure $\nu$ on an arbitrary measurable space, any finite index type (the empty one included) and a
  coordinatewise measurable field $H$ of positive semidefinite matrices, if every positive semidefinite $A$ has
  $x\mapsto\operatorname{tr}(AH(x)AH(x))$ integrable with $\int\operatorname{tr}(AHAH)\,d\nu\le2\operatorname{tr}(A^2)$,
  then every symmetric $B$ has $x\mapsto\operatorname{tr}(BHBH)$ integrable and
  $\int\operatorname{tr}(BHBH)\,d\nu\le2\operatorname{tr}(B^2)$.  The integrability for $B$ is derived; no finiteness of
  $\nu$, nonemptiness or commutation is assumed.  The positive semidefinite bound is a named hypothesis: it, the moment
  map, Brascamp--Lieb and Theorem~2.5 itself are not provided.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Write $B=P-N$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:letwin-symmetric-jordan}; pointwise $0\le\operatorname{tr}(BHBH)\le
  \operatorname{tr}(PHPH)+\operatorname{tr}(NHNH)$ gives integrability by domination and, integrated, the bound
  $2\operatorname{tr}(P^2)+2\operatorname{tr}(N^2)=2\operatorname{tr}(B^2)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Letwin: centered range density of compact smooth diffusion images (Lemma~A.6 support)]
  \label{lem:letwin-centered-range}
  \lean{FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.HeatSemigroup.compactSmoothDiffusionImage,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.HeatSemigroup.centered_mem_closure_compactSmoothDiffusionImage,
        FunctionalInequalities.KLS.LogConcave.HeatSemigroup.center_mem_closure_compactSmoothDiffusionImage}
  \leanok
  Letwin \cite{Letwin2026QuadraticKLS}, Lemma~A.6 (Brascamp--Lieb for $\nu$), supporting step for the finite-energy
  argument: for a log-concave probability law $\nu=m.\mathrm{tilted}(-U)$ on a finite-dimensional real inner product space,
  with $U\in C^1$ and $m$ an additive Haar measure, the images $-Lf$ of compactly supported smooth $f$ under the ordinary
  weighted diffusion operator are dense in the mean-zero classes of $L^2(\nu)$: every $g\in L^2(\nu)$ with mean $0$ lies in
  their closure, and so does the centering of every $g$.  The class $g$ is arbitrary (no operator-domain or compact-test
  restriction) and the density is derived, not assumed.  Not provided: Brascamp--Lieb itself, Lemma~A.6 with an infinite
  right side, the moment map and Theorem~2.5.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The kernel of the closed weighted operator $T$ consists of the constants, so a mean-zero $g$ is orthogonal to
